Figure 15
:
Schematics of the fast Fourier transform in bit-reversed order (FFTBR) and fast Walsh–Hadamard transform (FWHT). FFTBR is performed via an FFT decimation in time algorithm without the initial reordering of inputs. The inverse FFTBR (IFFTBR) may be performed by propagating
\{{\widetilde{y}}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
to
\{y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
in the other direction through the FFTBR algorithm.

17
Kernel Matrix Structures

This section will discuss the nice structures which appear in the kernel matrix of pairwise kernel evaluations at two sets of points when special kernels and point sets are used. The structures we present are generalizations of structures studied elsewhere in the literature where we additionally support kernel matrices formed from low-discrepancy sequences of potentially different sizes and with potentially different randomizations. This will later be exploited in
Section

24
for fast Gaussian process regression and in
Section

26
fast multitask Gaussian process regression where kernel matrices with different sequences of different sizes will be used for each task.

Throughout this section, let
{\mathsf{I}}_{r}
denote the size
2^{r}\times 2^{r}
identity matrix for
r\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. We will begin with a condition characterizing the fast transform matrices which we will later see are eigenvectors of nicely structured kernel matrices.

Condition 17.1
.

A sequence of matrices
\left({\mathsf{V}}_{m}\right)_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
satisfy this condition if for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
:

1.

{\mathsf{V}}_{m}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{m}\times 2^{m}}
.

2.

{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
is symmetric, i.e.,
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}^{\intercal}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}
, and unitary, i.e.,
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}{\mathsf{V}}_{m}={\mathsf{I}}_{m}
.

3.

The zeroth column of
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
is the constant vector
1/\sqrt{2^{m}}
.

4.

Computing
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\boldsymbol{y}}
costs
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{m}}
.

5.

For some
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{m}}
,

\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m+1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}&\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{w}}_{m})\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}\\
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}&-\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{w}}_{m})\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}\end{pmatrix}.

When point sets of different sizes are used, the resulting kernel matrices have blocks with nice structure. Here we prove a useful property for such kernel matrices.

Lemma 17.2
.

For
({\mathsf{V}}_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
satisfying
17.1
and any
p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
:

1.

If
p\geq q
and

{\mathsf{K}}:=({\mathsf{I}}_{p-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{p}\times 2^{q}}

for some
2^{p-q}\times 1
block matrix
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks, then

{\mathsf{K}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}

for some different
2^{p-q}\times 1
block matrix
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks.

2.

If
p\leq q
and

{\mathsf{K}}:={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}({\mathsf{I}}_{q-p}\otimes\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{p}\times 2^{q}}

for some
1\times 2^{q-p}
block matrix
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
with
2^{p}\times 2^{p}
diagonal blocks, then

{\mathsf{K}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}

for some different
1\times 2^{q-p}
block matrix
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
with
2^{p}\times 2^{p}
diagonal blocks.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume
p\geq q
. The result follows if we can show
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{p}}({\mathsf{I}}_{p-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q})
is a
2^{p-q}\times 2^{p-q}
block matrix with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks as this would imply

{\mathsf{K}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{p}}({\mathsf{I}}_{p-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}

for some different
2^{p-q}\times 1
block matrix
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks. We show this by induction. This clearly holds when
p=q
. Suppose this holds for all
p\in\{q,\dots,m\}
with
m>q
, and denote the
2^{m-q}\times 2^{m-q}
block matrix with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks by
{\mathsf{D}}=\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}({\mathsf{I}}_{m-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q})
. Then
17.1
implies

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m+1}}({\mathsf{I}}_{m+1-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}&\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{w}}_{m})\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}\\
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}&-\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{w}}_{m})\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{I}}_{m-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q}&\\
&{\mathsf{I}}_{m-q}\otimes{\mathsf{V}}_{q}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{D}}&\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m}){\mathsf{D}}\\
{\mathsf{D}}&-\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m}){\mathsf{D}}\end{pmatrix}

which is a
2^{m+1-q}\times 2^{m+1-q}
block matrix with
2^{q}\times 2^{q}
diagonal blocks, so the result follows.
∎

Now, let us characterize a special collection of two point sets, a kernel, and a set of fast transform matrices.

Condition 17.3
.

A pair of sequences
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}},({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}
, a SPSD kernel
K:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and a set of matrices
\left({\mathsf{V}}_{m}\right)_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
satisfy this condition if
17.1
holds, and for any
p,q,r,c\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
it holds that

\left(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime})\right)_{i=r2^{p},k=c2^{q}}^{(r+1)2^{p},(c+1)2^{q}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{p}\times 2^{q}}

for some
2^{p-\min(p,q)}\times 2^{q-\min(p,q)}
block matrix
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
with
2^{\min(p,q)}\times 2^{\min(p,q)}
diagonal blocks. Note that
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
implicitly depends on
p
,
q
,
r
, and
c
while
{\mathsf{V}}_{p}
only depends on
p
.

The following theorem shows how to exploit such collections for fast kernel computations.

Theorem 17.4
.

Under
17.3
, suppose

{\mathsf{K}}={\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{p}\times 2^{q}}

for some
2^{p-\min(p,q)}\times 2^{q-\min(p,q)}
block matrix
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
with
2^{\min(p,q)}\times 2^{\min(p,q)}
diagonal blocks. Then
{\mathsf{K}}
is fully specified by the
2^{\max(p,q)}
entries of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
across all diagonal blocks. Moreover, these entries
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{\max(p,q)}}
can be computed at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{\max(p,q)}\max(p,q))
cost with only
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{\max(p,q)})
storage using the following formulas.

1.

If
p\geq q
, then
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sqrt{2^{q}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{p}}{\mathsf{K}}_{:,0}
.

2.

If
q\geq p
, then
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sqrt{2^{p}}\;{\mathsf{V}}_{q}{\mathsf{K}}_{0,:}^{\intercal}
.

Here
{\mathsf{K}}_{:,0}
and
{\mathsf{K}}_{0,:}
are the zeroth column and row of
{\mathsf{K}}
respectively.

Proof.

If
p\geq q
,

{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}={\mathsf{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{q}=\sqrt{2^{q}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{p}}{\mathsf{V}}_{p}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{1}}=\sqrt{2^{q}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{p}}{\mathsf{K}}_{:,0}.

If
q\geq p
, then

{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\intercal}=({\boldsymbol{1}}_{p}{\mathsf{\Lambda}})^{\intercal}=\sqrt{2^{p}}({\boldsymbol{v}}_{1}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{q}}{\mathsf{V}}_{q})^{\intercal}=\sqrt{2^{p}}({\mathsf{K}}_{0,:}{\mathsf{V}}_{q})^{\intercal}=\sqrt{2^{p}}{\mathsf{V}}_{q}{\mathsf{K}}_{0,:}^{\intercal}.

∎

Corollary 17.5
.

Under
17.3
, if
{\mathsf{K}}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}{\mathsf{V}}_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{m}\times 2^{m}}
then one can compute
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\sqrt{2^{m}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}{\mathsf{K}}_{:,0}
at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
cost and with
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m})
storage. One may also perform a matrix-vector multiplication, solve a linear system (assuming
{\mathsf{K}}
SPD), and compute the determinant of
{\mathsf{K}}
at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
cost using

{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}\odot{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}),\qquad{\mathsf{K}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}./{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}),\qquad\lvert{\mathsf{K}}\rvert=\prod_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}\lambda_{i}

where
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}
,
\odot
denotes the elementwise (Hadamard) product, and
./
denotes the elementwise division.

17.1
SI Kernels, Rank-1 Lattices, and the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT)

Definition 17.6
.

A function
K:[0,1)^{d}\times[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is shift-invariant (SI) if for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=Q({\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\bmod 1)

for some
Q:[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

Suppose
n=2^{m}
for some
m\geq 0
and let
W_{m}=\exp(-2\pi\sqrt{-1}/2^{m})
. Then we will denote the scaled Fourier matrix by
\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}=\{W_{m}^{ij}/\sqrt{2^{m}}\}_{i,j=0}^{2^{m}-1}
which satisfies
{\mathsf{F}}_{m}\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}={\mathsf{I}}_{m}
. We will call
\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}
and
{\mathsf{F}}_{m}
the DFT (discrete Fourier transform) and IDFT (inverse DFT) matrices respectively. Multiplication by
\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}
and
{\mathsf{F}}_{m}
can each be performed at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)={\mathcal{O}}(n\log n)
cost using the FFT (fast Fourier transform) and IFFT (inverse FFT) respectively
[
101
]
. This section will use shifted rank-1 lattices which were detailed in
Section

11.1
.

Lemma 17.7
.

The following holds for any shifted rank-1 lattices in linear order

({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}i/2^{m}+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}i/2^{m}+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating vector
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
. When
K
is a SPSD SI kernel, the following eigendecomposition holds

(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i,k=0}^{2^{m}-1}={\mathsf{F}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}.

Proof.

For this proof only, denote
\{{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\}_{p}:=({\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\bmod p
. Then with
n=2^{m}
,

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}\}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\{{\boldsymbol{g}}i/n+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\}_{1}-\{{\boldsymbol{g}}k/n+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}_{1}\}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\{{\boldsymbol{g}}\{i-k\}_{n}/n+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\}_{1}-\{{\boldsymbol{g}}0/n+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}_{1}\}_{1}=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\{i-k\}_{n}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime}\}_{1}.

Since
K
is SI, we see
K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime})=K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\{i-k\}_{n}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime})
, so the matrix is circulant. The eigendecomposition of a circulant matrix in terms of the DFT and IDFT matrices is well known, see for example
[
194
]
.
∎

Figure

16
visualizes lattices in linear order, SI kernels, and the resulting circulant kernel matrices. Let
R_{m}(i)
reverse the first
m
bits of
0\leq i<2^{m}
in base
b=2
, i.e., if
i=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}{\mathsf{i}}_{k}2^{k}
then
R(i)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}{\mathsf{i}}_{m-k-1}2^{k}
. Notice that
v(i)=R_{m}(i)/n
where
v(i)
is the van der Corput sequence defined in (
2
). Let
{\mathsf{P}}_{m}
be a permutation matrix with the property that
{\mathsf{P}}_{m}^{\intercal}(y_{i})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}=(y_{R(i)})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
bit-reverses the ordering. Let us denote
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}:=\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}{\mathsf{P}}_{m}^{\intercal}
and
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}={\mathsf{P}}_{m}{\mathsf{F}}_{m}
, which we will call the DFTBR (DFT in bit-reversed order) and inverse IDFTBR (inverse DFTBR) matrices respectively. Multiplication by
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}
may be done by performing a bit-reversal at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m})
cost and then applying the FFT algorithm at costs
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
. This algorithm, which we call the FFTBR (FFT bit-reversed), is visualized in
Figure

15
. Similarly, multiplication by
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
may be done by applying the IFFT algorithm at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
cost and then performing a bit-reversal at
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m})
cost. We call this algorithm the IFFTBR (IFFT bit-reversed). Better yet, the FFTBR algorithm is equivalent to a decimation-in-time FFT without the initial bit-reversal step, and likewise for the IFFTBR.

Lemma 17.8
.

The DFTBR matrices
\left({\mathsf{V}}_{m}\right)_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
satisfy
17.1
with
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m}=\left(\exp(-\pi\sqrt{-1}i/2^{m})\right)_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
.

Proof.

The first four properties immediately follow from the prior definitions and discussion. For the last property, notice that
\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m+1}}=\left(W_{m+1}^{iR_{m+1}(j)}/\sqrt{2^{m+1}}\right)_{i,j=0}^{2^{m+1}-1}
. For
0\leq j<2^{m}
we have
R_{m+1}(j)=2R_{m}(j)
so
W^{iR_{m+1}(j)}_{m+1}=W^{iR_{m}(j)}_{m}
. For
2^{m}\leq j<2^{m+1}
we have
R_{m+1}(j)=2R_{m}(j-2^{m})+1
so
W^{iR_{m+1}(j)}_{m+1}=W_{m}^{iR(j-2^{m})}W_{m+1}^{i}
. For
0\leq i<2^{m}
we have
W_{m+1}^{2^{m}+i}=-W_{m+1}^{i}
. Notice that
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m+1}
is the
\alpha=2
interlacing of
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m}
and
W_{m+1}{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m}
.
∎

Lemma 17.9
.

The following holds for any shifted rank-1 lattices in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating vector
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
. When
K
is a SPSD SI kernel we have the following eigendecomposition

(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i,k=0}^{2^{m}-1}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}.

Proof.

Lemma

17.7
and the definition of
{\mathsf{P}}_{m}
directly imply
{\mathsf{P}}_{m}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{K}}{\mathsf{P}}_{m}={\mathsf{F}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{F}}_{m}}
from which the theorem follows.
∎

Lemma 17.10
.

The following holds for any shifted rank-1 lattices in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating vector
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
. For any
m,r,c\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, when
K
is a SPSD SI kernel we have the following eigendecomposition

(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i=r2^{m},k=c2^{m}}^{(r+1)2^{m}-1,(c+1)2^{m}-1}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}

Proof.

For
r2^{m}\leq i<(r+1)2^{m}
we have
v(i)=v(i-r2^{m})+v(r2^{m})
, so
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=r2^{m}}^{(r+1)2^{m}-1}=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})\bmod 1)_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
where
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}=({\boldsymbol{\Delta}}+{\boldsymbol{g}}v(r2^{m}))\bmod 1
, i.e.,
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=r2^{m}}^{(r+1)2^{m}-1}
is
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
after a shift by
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}
and modulo 1. Similarly, for
c2^{m}\leq i<(c+1)2^{m}
we have
v(i)=v(i-c2^{m})+v(c2^{m})
, so
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=c2^{m}}^{(c+1)2^{m}-1}=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})\bmod 1)_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
where
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}=({\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}+{\boldsymbol{g}}v(c2^{m}))\bmod 1
, i.e.,
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=c2^{m}}^{(c+1)2^{m}-1}
is
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
after a shift by
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}
and modulo 1. The result then follows from
Lemma

17.9
.
∎

Theorem 17.11
.

17.3
is satisfied for DFTBR matrices
\left({\mathsf{V}}_{m}\right)_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
, a SPSD SI kernel
K:[0,1)^{d}\times[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and any shifted rank-1 lattices in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime}):=(({\boldsymbol{g}}v(i)+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})\bmod 1)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating vector
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of combining
Lemma

17.10
with
Lemma

17.2
.
∎

17.2
DSI Kernels, Digital Nets, and the Fast Walsh–Hadamard Transform (FWHT)

Definition 17.12
.

For a fixed prime base
b
, denote the digit-wise addition and subtraction of
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
by

x\oplus x^{\prime}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(({\mathsf{x}}_{\ell}+{\mathsf{x}}^{\prime}_{\ell})\bmod b)2^{-\ell-1}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad x\ominus y=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(({\mathsf{x}}_{\ell}-{\mathsf{x}}^{\prime}_{\ell})\bmod b)2^{-\ell-1}

where
x=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}{\mathsf{x}}_{\ell}2^{\ell}
and
x^{\prime}=\sum_{\ell\geq 0}{\mathsf{x}}_{\ell}^{\prime}2^{\ell}
. When applied to vectors, these operations are applied elementwise, i.e., for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}

{\boldsymbol{x}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}=(x_{1}\oplus x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}\oplus x_{d}^{\prime})\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad{\boldsymbol{x}}\ominus{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}=(x_{1}\ominus x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}\ominus x_{d}^{\prime}).

We technically require the base
b
expansions of
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
do not end in infinite trails of the digit
b-1
. This will be assumed throughout, and in not an issue for our finite precision implementation.

Definition 17.13
.

A function
K:[0,1)^{d}\times[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) if for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=Q({\boldsymbol{x}}\ominus{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})

for some
Q:[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
n=2^{m}
where
m\geq 0
. The
2^{m}\times 2^{m}
scaled Hadamard matrix
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
may be defined recursively starting from
{\mathsf{V}}_{0}=(1)
and using the relation

{\mathsf{V}}_{m+1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{m},&{\mathsf{V}}_{m}\\
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}&-{\mathsf{V}}_{m}\end{pmatrix}.

Multiplication by the scaled Hadamard matrix
{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
can be performed in
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m}m)
computations using the fast Walsh–Hadamard transform (FWHT)
[
102
]
visualized in
Figure

15
.

Lemma 17.14
.

The real-valued scaled Hadamard matrices
(V_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
satisfy

17.1
with
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{m}={\boldsymbol{1}}_{m}
the constant length
2^{m}
vector of ones.

Proof.

These properties immediately follow from the prior definitions and discussion.
∎

This section will use the digitally-shifted digital nets discussed in
Section

11.2
. Here we will only consider base
b=2
digital nets for which digit-wise addition and subtraction are equivalent and may be computed quickly using efficient XOR (exclusive or) operations. The theory should readily extend to digital nets with arbitrary prime base
b
.

Let us denote the generating matrices by
{\mathsf{G}}\in[0,1)^{d\times\infty}
, where the generating matrix
{\mathsf{C}}_{j}\in\{0,1\}^{t_{\max}\times m}
for
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
presented in
Section

11.2
is now identified with the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
row of
{\mathsf{G}}
by letting
m\to\infty
and writing each column of
{\mathsf{C}}_{j}
in a floating point representation. We will denote the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
column of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, e.g., we denote the zeroth column of
{\mathsf{G}}
by
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{0}
.

Note that
{\mathsf{G}}
may be the result of applying linear matrix scrambling (LMS) to some original generating matrices, but the following theory does not permit different LMSs for each sequence, i.e., the same
{\mathsf{G}}
must be used for each sequence. Higher-order digital nets and higher-order scrambling with LMS is permitted under the same restrictions. Nested uniform scrambling (NUS) is not permitted.

Recall that we write the binary expansion of
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
as
i=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}2^{\ell}
.

Lemma 17.15
.

The following holds for any digitally-shifted base
2
digital nets in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating matrices
{\mathsf{G}}\in[0,1)^{d\times\infty}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
. When
K
is a SPSD DSI kernel we have the following eigendecomposition

(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i,k=0}^{2^{m}-1}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}{\mathsf{V}}_{m}.

(23)

Proof.

This clearly holds for
m=0
. Assume this holds for
m\in\{0,\dots,j\}
with
j>0
, and let us denote

{\mathsf{K}}:=(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i,k=0}^{2^{j+1}-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{K}}_{00}&{\mathsf{K}}_{01}\\
{\mathsf{K}}_{10}&{\mathsf{K}}_{11}\end{pmatrix}.

The eigendecomposition
{\mathsf{K}}_{00}={\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{00}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}
holds by assumption. Notice that

\displaystyle(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})_{i=2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}-1}

\displaystyle=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})_{i=0}^{2^{j}-1},\qquad{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}={\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{g}}_{j},\qquad\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})_{i=2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}-1}

\displaystyle=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})_{i=0}^{2^{j}-1},\qquad{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}={\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\oplus{\boldsymbol{g}}_{j}.

Then the assumption and the digitally-shift-invariant property imply that
{\mathsf{K}}_{01}={\mathsf{K}}_{10}={\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{01}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}
and
{\mathsf{K}}_{11}={\mathsf{K}}_{00}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{V}}_{j+1}{\mathsf{K}}{\mathsf{V}}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{j},&{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\\
{\mathsf{V}}_{j}&-{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{00}{\mathsf{V}}_{j},&{\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{01}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\\
{\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{01}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}&{\mathsf{V}}_{j}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{00}{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{j},&{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\\
{\mathsf{V}}_{j}&-{\mathsf{V}}_{j}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{00}+{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{10}&\\
&{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{00}-{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{01}\end{pmatrix},

which completes the proof by induction. A more detailed discussion and proof can be found in
[
72
, Section 5.3.2]
.
∎

Kernel matrices taking the form of (
23
) are said to be RSBT (recursive symmetric block Toeplitz).
Figure

16
visualizes base 2 digital nets in radical inverse order, DSI kernels, and the resulting RSBT kernel matrices.

Lemma 17.16
.

The following holds for any digitally-shifted base
2
digital nets in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating matrices
{\mathsf{G}}\in[0,1)^{d\times\infty}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
. For any
m,r,c\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, when
K
is a SPSD DSI kernel we have the following eigendecomposition

(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\prime}))_{i=r2^{m},k=c2^{m}}^{(r+1)2^{m}-1,(c+1)2^{m}-1}={\mathsf{V}}_{m}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})_{i=r2^{m}}^{(r+1)2^{m}-1}

\displaystyle=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1},\quad{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}=\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{j}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},\quad j=r2^{m},

\displaystyle(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})_{i=c2^{m}}^{(c+1)2^{m}-1}

\displaystyle=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1},\quad{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}=\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{j}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime},\quad j=c2^{m}.

The result then follows from
Lemma

17.15
.
∎

Theorem 17.17
.

17.3
is satisfied for scaled Hadamard matrices
\left({\mathsf{V}}_{m}\right)_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
, a SPSD DSI kernel
K:[0,1)^{d}\times[0,1)^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and any digitally-shifted base
2
digital nets in radical inverse order
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}:=(\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{i}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\ell}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with common generating matrices
{\mathsf{G}}\in[0,1)^{d\times\infty}
and any shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of combining
Lemma

17.16
with
Lemma

17.2
.
∎

Figure 16
:
Lattice points in linear order paired with shift-invariant (SI) kernels produce circulant kernel (Gram) matrices. Base
2
digital nets in radical inverse order paired with digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernels produce RSBT (recursive symmetric block Toeplitz) Gram matrices. Circulant and RSBT matrices have eigendecompositions with nicely structured eigenvector matrices which enable fast Gram matrix computations.

18
Kernel Forms

Here we will discuss how the special multivariate kernels we use for fast kernel computations are built from univariate kernel forms. The resulting mixture-product kernels and product kernels have been considered elsewhere in the literature. We provide code examples using these multivariate kernels for the fast kernel matrix computations discussed in
Section

17
. Univariate SI kernels are discussed in
Section

19
and DSI kernels in
Section

20
.

18.1
Multivarite Kernel Decompositions

In this section we will discuss kernels of varying smoothness which are SI (
Definition

17.6
) or DSI (
Definition

17.13
). Let us motivate our kernels forms with the following functional ANOVA decomposition
[
50
, Appendix A]
.

Remark 18.1
.

If
{\mathbb{V}}[f({\boldsymbol{X}})]<\infty
for
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}([0,1]^{d})
, then

f({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}}f_{\mathfrak{u}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),\quad f_{\mathfrak{u}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f({\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-{\mathfrak{u}}}-\sum_{{\mathfrak{v}}\subsetneq{\mathfrak{u}}}f_{\mathfrak{v}}({\boldsymbol{x}}),\quad f_{\emptyset}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f({\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}

where
f_{\mathfrak{u}}
only depends on
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\mathfrak{u}}:=(x_{j})_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}
, not
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-{\mathfrak{u}}}:=(x_{j})_{j\notin{\mathfrak{u}}}
.

Recall sums and products of kernels are still kernels
[
53
]
, see also
[
195
, Chapter 2]
for a discussion on expressing structure through combining kernels. Our SI and DSI kernels will be products and sums of one-dimensional SI and DSI kernels. Specifically, for some compatible smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, some weights
({\widetilde{\eta}}_{\mathfrak{u}})_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, and a global scaling parameter
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, we will consider multivariate
mixture-product kernels
of the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{\mathfrak{u}}\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})

(24)

where the product over the empty set is taken to be
1
, and we require
{\widetilde{\eta}}_{\emptyset}=1
.

Product kernels
are a special case of mixture-product kernels (
24
) where
{\widetilde{\eta}}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}\eta_{j}
for some per-dimension weights
\{\eta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
(which we may also call lengthscales) so that

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left[1+\eta_{j}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right].

(25)

Product kernels, while less expressive, only cost
{\mathcal{O}}(d)
to evaluate compared to the
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{d})
cost for mixture-product kernels, assuming the one-dimensional kernels cost
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
to evaluate.
[
65
]
proposes serendipitous weights, a special form of product weights, which showed unexpectedly strong performance for fast lattice based kernel interpolation.

The next two lemmas will consider derivatives and integrals of mixture-product and product kernels respectively. Such derivatives and integrals will appear later when considering derivative-informed Gaussian process regression (
Section

27
) and Bayesian cubature (
Section

25.4
and
Section

26.4
). For
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
we will denote the support by
S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})=\{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}:\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}>0\}
with cardinality
\lvert S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})\rvert
.

Lemma 18.2
.

For compatible
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
, the mixture-product kernels (
24
) satisfy

\displaystyle K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\left(\prod_{j\in S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}\setminus S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{{\mathfrak{u}}\cup S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\gamma\left(\prod_{j\in S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}\setminus S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{{\mathfrak{u}}\cup S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}=\gamma\left(\prod_{j\in S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}\setminus S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{{\mathfrak{u}}\cup S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{\prime}\right)

whenever the appropriate kernel derivatives exist. Assuming the cost of evaluating the one-dimensional kernels and their integrals are all
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, then the cost of evaluating any of the above expressions is
{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})\rvert+2^{d-\lvert S({\boldsymbol{\beta}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})\rvert})
.

Lemma 18.3
.

For compatible
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}
, the product kernels (
25
) satisfy

\displaystyle K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\gamma\prod_{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\left(1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}+\eta_{j}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}=

\displaystyle\gamma\prod_{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\left(1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}+\eta_{j}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\gamma\prod_{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\left(1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}+\eta_{j}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha_{j}}^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{\prime}\right)

where
1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}
is
1
if
\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0
and
0
otherwise.

18.2
Examples of Fast Kernel Computations

The following codes perform fast kernel computations with the kernel (Gram) matrix

{\mathsf{K}}=(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i^{\prime}}))_{i,i^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}.

Specifically, we evaluate the kernel matrix product
{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{u}}
and solve the linear system
{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{v}}={\boldsymbol{y}}
for
{\boldsymbol{v}}
, each at
{\mathcal{O}}(n\log n)
cost and requiring only
{\mathcal{O}}(n)
storage following
Corollary

17.5
. We also give an example of how to compute
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}_{\mathrm{full}}=\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m+1}}\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}\\
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathrm{new}}\end{pmatrix}
given
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}=\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{m}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
for special fast transform matrices satisfying
17.1
. This is useful for efficient updates after doubling the sample size while retaining the original points.
