The following subsections will define various univariate SI (
Section

19
) and DSI (
Section

20
) kernels
K_{\alpha}
which are used to construct the multivariate kernels above. While the univariate kernels we present are SPSD, the multivariate mixture-product or product kernels will be SPD as we always add
\gamma>0
(corresponding to the
{\mathfrak{u}}=\emptyset
term in (
24
)). Again, we will consider derivatives and integrals of univariate kernels which will be useful later when considering derivative-informed Gaussian process regression (
Section

27
) and Bayesian cubature (
Section

25.4
and
Section

26.4
). Moreover, the univariate SI and DSI kernels we present will all satisfy

\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}K_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0

for all
x\in[0,1)
and all compatible
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.

19
Shift-Invariant (SI) Kernels

Here we present weighted Korobov spaces which have been extensively studied throughout the literature
[
64
,
65
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
]
. To the best of our knowledge, the kernel derivative forms have not appeared elsewhere in the literature.

19.1
Fourier series of smooth functions

Assume
f:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
has an absolutely convergent Fourier series

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\widehat{f}}(k)e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}kx},\qquad{\widehat{f}}(k)=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)e^{-2\pi\sqrt{-1}kx}\mathrm{d}x

where
{\widehat{f}}(k)
are Fourier coefficients. For
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, if
f^{(\beta)}(x)
has an absolutely convergent Fourier series and
f^{(\beta^{\prime})}
is periodic for all
\beta^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,\beta-1\}
, then

f^{(\beta)}(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\widehat{f}}(k)(2\pi\sqrt{-1}k)^{\beta}e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}kx}.

This implies the following relationship between Fourier coefficients of the function and Fourier coefficients of its derivative:

\widehat{f^{(\beta)}}(k)=(2\pi\sqrt{-1}k)^{\beta}{\widehat{f}}(k).

19.2
Periodic Sobolev Spaces

Definition 19.1
.

Suppose
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}
. We define the weight
{\widetilde{r}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

{\widetilde{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k):=k^{-2\alpha},

and define our SPSD SI kernel
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}{\widetilde{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}k(x-x^{\prime})}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}{\widetilde{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\cos(2\pi k(x-x^{\prime})).

Theorem 19.2
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
suppose the real-valued functions
f
and
g
have absolutely convergent Fourier series and both
f^{(\beta)}
and
g^{(\beta)}
are periodic for all
\beta\in\{1,\dots,\alpha-1\}
. Then for the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}}:=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}{\widetilde{r}}_{\alpha}(k)^{-2}{\widehat{f}}_{k}\overline{{\widehat{g}}_{k}}

(26)

we have

\langle f,g\rangle_{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}}=(-1)^{\alpha}(2\pi)^{-2\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\alpha)}(x)g^{(\alpha)}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}k^{2\alpha}{\widehat{f}}(k)\overline{{\widehat{g}}(k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{-2\alpha}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}(k)}\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(k)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\alpha}(2\pi)^{-2\alpha}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}}(k)\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(k)}

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\alpha)}(x)g^{(\alpha)}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}}(k)e^{-2\pi kx}\right)\left(\sum_{h\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(h)e^{-2\pi kx}}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,h\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}}(k)\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(h)}\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\pi ix(k-h)}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,h\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}}(k)\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(h)}\delta_{k,h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\widehat{f^{(\alpha)}}(k)\overline{\widehat{g^{(\alpha)}}(k)}.

∎

Remark 19.3
.

For
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, the RKHS with mixture-product kernels (
24
) formed from univariate SI kernels
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha_{j}}
is a weighted periodic unanchored Sobolev space of dominating smoothness of order
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
with RKHS norm

\lVert f\rVert^{2}:=\sum_{{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2\lvert{\mathfrak{u}}\rvert}\gamma_{\mathfrak{u}}}\int_{[0,1]^{\lvert{\mathfrak{u}}\rvert}}\left\lvert\int_{[0,1]^{s-\lvert{\mathfrak{u}}\rvert}}\left(\prod_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{j}}}{\partial y_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}}\right)f({\boldsymbol{y}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{-{\mathfrak{u}}}\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathfrak{u}}.

Here
f:[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathfrak{u}}=({\boldsymbol{y}}_{j})_{j\in{\mathfrak{u}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{-{\mathfrak{u}}}:=(y_{j})_{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}\setminus{\mathfrak{u}}}
, and
\lvert{\mathfrak{u}}\rvert
is the cardinality of
{\mathfrak{u}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
. This is a special case of the weighted Korobov space which allows for real smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}
as we have discussed throughout this section.

Theorem 19.4
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}
. Suppose
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
satisfy
2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime}>1
. Then,
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
is also a SPSD SI kernel which may be written as

{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,y)=(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\beta^{\prime}}{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha-\beta/2-\beta^{\prime}/2}(x,y).

Moreover, for any
x\in[0,1)
,

\int_{0}^{1}{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0.

Proof.

{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}
is real-valued since
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}
is real-valued. The first assertion follows from

{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,y)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}(2\pi\sqrt{-1}k)^{\beta}r(k)e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}kx}\overline{r(k)(2\pi\sqrt{-1}k)^{\beta^{\prime}}e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}ky}}.

The integral forms follow from the fact that for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}
we have
\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}kx}=0
.
∎

Theorem 19.5
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
satisfying
2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime}>1
, we have that for any
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)

{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{(-1)^{\alpha+\beta^{\prime}+1}(2\pi)^{2\alpha}}{(2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime})!}B_{2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime}}(x-x^{\prime}\bmod 1)

where
B_{p}
is the
p^{\mathrm{th}}
Bernoulli polynomial.

Theorem 19.6
.

For
\alpha>{\mathbb{R}}_{>3/2}
, both of the following limits exist and are finite:

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}},

\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{2},x)+{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}

\displaystyle\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(0,0)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(h,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h^{2}}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1-\cos(2\pi kh)}{k^{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1}{k^{2\alpha}}\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1-\cos(2\pi kh)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1}{k^{2(\alpha-1)}}

and

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}

\displaystyle\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{2},x)+{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{1}-h_{2},0)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{1},0)-{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{2},0)+{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}(0,0)}{h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{1}{h_{1}h_{2}}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{\cos(2\pi k(h_{1}-h_{2}))-\cos(2\pi kh_{1})-\cos(2\pi kh_{2})+1}{k^{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1}{k^{2\alpha}}\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{\cos(2\pi k(h_{1}-h_{2}))-\cos(2\pi kh_{1})-\cos(2\pi kh_{2})+1}{h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1}{k^{2(\alpha-1)}}.

We have passed the limits into the sum because the sum is absolutely convergent when
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}
. Moreover, both final expressions are proportional to
\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{0}}\frac{1}{k^{2(\alpha-1)}}
, which is finite whenever
\alpha>3/2
.
∎

20
Digitally-Shift-Invariant (DSI) Kernels

Here we will present classes of DSI kernels. An existing class of low-order smoothness DSI kernels is studied in
[
161
]
. Here we derive new higher-order smoothness DSI kernels which exploit the decay of Walsh coefficients of smooth functions as studied in
[
167
]
. The forms of our new higher-order smoothness DSI kernels have appeared in worst case error bounds for Quasi-Monte Carlo methods using polynomial lattice rules
[
162
]
, but there they were not interpreted kernels. We also derive a new expression for a certain
\alpha=4
higher-order smoothness DSI kernels which has not appeared elsewhere in the literature.

In what follows, we will assume
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a fixed prime base. Let
\beta(x)
be the power of
b^{-1}
of the first non-zero digit in the base
b
expansion of
x\in[0,1)
, i.e.,

\beta(x):=-\lfloor\log_{b}(x)\rfloor.

Definition 20.1
.

For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
with
k=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{k}}_{\ell}b^{\ell}
and any
x\in[0,1)
with
x=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathsf{x}}_{\ell}b^{-\ell}\in[0,1)
, define the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
Walsh function

\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{b}\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathsf{k}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{x}}_{\ell+1}\right).

Lemma 20.2
.

Let
k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
x,y\in[0,1)
, with the restriction that if
x,y
are not
b
-adic rationals then
x\oplus y
and
x\ominus y
are not allowed to be a
b
-adic rationals. Then

1.

\mathrm{wal}_{k}(0)=1.

2.

\displaystyle\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\mathrm{wal}_{h}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{wal}_{k\oplus h}(x),\qquad\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{h}(x)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{wal}_{k\ominus h}(x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\oplus y),\qquad\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{k}(y)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus y).

3.

\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\begin{cases}1,&k=0\\
0,&k>0\end{cases}.

4.

\left\{\mathrm{wal}_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\right\}
is a complete orthonormal system in
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}([0,1))
, the space of square integrable functions.

5.

For any
f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{2}([0,1))
we have

\int_{0}^{1}f(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{0}^{1}f(x\oplus\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{0}^{1}f(x\ominus\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma.

6.

\sum_{k=0}^{b^{a}-1}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}b^{a},&a\leq\beta(x)-1,\\
0,&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Proof.

These properties are well known and stated in many papers, e.g.,
[
196
, Proposition 3]
. Most proofs are straightforward, while others can be found in
[
162
]
or
[
197
]
or
[
198
]
.
∎

For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
write
k=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\#k}{\mathsf{k}}_{a_{\ell}}b^{a_{\ell}}
where
a_{1}>\dots>a_{\#k}\geq 0
and
{\mathsf{k}}_{a_{\ell}}\in\{1,\dots,b-1\}
for
1\leq\ell\leq\#k
. Here
\#k
is the number of non-zero elements in the base
b
expansion of
k
. For
k=0
, set
\#k=0
and set the empty sum to
0
as well. Moreover, we may set
k^{\prime}=k-{\mathsf{k}}_{a_{1}}b^{a_{1}}
.

Definition 20.3
.

We define the Dick weight functions for
\alpha,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
as

\mu_{\alpha}(k)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\min(\alpha,\#k)}(a_{\ell}+1),\qquad{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)=\mu_{\alpha}(k)+\max(\alpha-\#k,0)(a_{\#k}+1)

where
\mu_{\alpha}(0)=\mu_{0}(k)={\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(0)={\mathring{\mu}}_{0}(k)=0
. These are used extensively in
[
167
]
which provides many of the inequalities used in the following subsections.

20.1
RKHS Preliminaries for Higher-Order Smoothness DSI Kernels

For
x\in[0,1)
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
define the Walsh coefficient

{\widehat{f}}(k)=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)}\mathrm{d}x.

If
f
has an absolutely convergent Walsh series representation then

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\widehat{f}}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

For any kernel
K:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we will denote its Walsh coefficient at
k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
(when it exists) by

{\mathcal{K}}(k,h):=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}K(x,x^{\prime})\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)}\mathrm{wal}_{h}(x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x.

We will carry accents through this notation as well, e.g.,
{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}(k,h)
is the Walsh coefficient corresponding to
{\widetilde{K}}
.

Definition 20.4
.

Following
[
161
, Defintion 6]
, for any SPSD kernel
K:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the associated SPSD DSI kernel for any
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
to be

K^{\mathrm{DSI}}(x,x^{\prime}):=\int_{0}^{1}K(x\oplus\sigma,x^{\prime}\oplus\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Lemma 20.5
.

For
K^{\mathrm{DSI}}
in
Definition

20.4
, if
{\mathcal{K}}(k,0)={\mathcal{K}}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, then

K^{\mathrm{DSI}}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime}),\qquad({\mathcal{K}}(k,k))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

Following
[
161
, Lemma 5]
, we have that

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{DSI}}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}K(x\oplus\sigma,x^{\prime}\oplus\sigma)\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{K}}(k,h)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\oplus\sigma)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{h}(x^{\prime}\oplus\sigma)}\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{K}}(k,h)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x^{\prime})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(\sigma)\overline{\mathrm{wal}_{h}(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime})

where
{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\in{\mathbb{R}}
follows from
K(x,x^{\prime})=\overline{K(x^{\prime},x)}
.
∎

Lemma 20.6
.

If a SPSD DSI kernel
K:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
can be written as

K(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}r^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime})\qquad\forall\;x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)

for some
r:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, then the RKHS of
K
can be written as

H(K)=\left\{f:[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}\;|\quad\;\exists\;(w_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),\quad f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}r(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\right\}.

Theorem 20.7
.

Suppose we are given a SPSD kernel
K
and construct
K^{\mathrm{DSI}}
as in
Definition

20.4
. Moreover, suppose we construct the kernel

R(x,y)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}r^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime})

by choosing
(r(k))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
so that
R
is SPSD.

1.

If there is some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\leq Cr^{2}(k)
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
H(K^{\mathrm{DSI}})\subseteq H(R)
.

2.

If there is some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\geq Cr^{2}(k)
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
H(R)\subseteq H(K^{\mathrm{DSI}})
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

20.6
we have

H(R)=\left\{f:[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}\;|\;\exists\;(w_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}r(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\right\}\qquad\text{and}

H(K^{\mathrm{DSI}})=\left\{f:[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}\;|\;\exists\;(v_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}}),f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}v_{k}\sqrt{{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\right\}

where
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
is the space of sequences
(x_{k})_{k\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\sum_{k\geq 0}\lvert x_{k}\rvert^{2}<\infty
.

For the first claim, if
f\in H(K^{\mathrm{DSI}})
then for some
(v_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
we have

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}v_{k}\sqrt{{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

Define
w_{k}=v_{k}\sqrt{{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)}/r(k)
so that

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}r(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

By the assumption, there is some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)\leq Cr^{2}(k)
which implies
w_{k}\leq\sqrt{C}v_{k}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. This shows
(w_{k})\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
and thus
f\in H(R)
.

For the second claim, if
f\in H(R)
then for some
(w_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
we have

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}r(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

Define
v_{k}=w_{k}r(k)/\sqrt{{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)}
so that

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}v_{k}\sqrt{{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

By the assumption, there is some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
such that
r^{2}(k)\leq C{\mathcal{K}}(k,k)
which implies
v_{k}\leq\sqrt{C}w_{k}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. This shows
(v_{k})\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
and thus
f\in H(K^{\mathrm{DSI}})
.
∎

20.2
DSI Kernels for Smooth Periodic Functions

Recall that for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}
, the SPSD kernel
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}
in
Definition

19.1
corresponds to the weighted Korobov RKHS of smooth periodic functions.

Lemma 20.8
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,0)={\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and there is some
{\mathring{C}}_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,k)\leq{\mathring{C}}_{\alpha}b^{-2{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}.

Proof.

This is a weaker version of
[
196
, Lemma 19]
which gives explicit bounds on
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,h)\rvert
for any
k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Lemma 20.9
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
,
f\in H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
, and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there is some
{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptC}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that

\left\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\right\rvert\leq{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptC}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}.

Proof.

[
167
, Theorem 16]
gives an explicit constant
{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptC}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}
.
∎

Definition 20.10
.

Fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
. We define the weight
{\mathring{r}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

{\mathring{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)=b^{-2{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)},

and define our SPSD DSI kernel
{\mathring{K}}_{\alpha}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{\mathring{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathring{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime}).

Definition 20.11
.

Fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
. We define the weight
{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)=b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}

and define our SPSD DSI kernel
{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime}).

Theorem 20.12
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
we have

H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{DSI}}\right)\subseteq H({\mathring{K}}_{\alpha})\subseteq H({{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha})

and

H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq H({{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}).

Proof.

We have
H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{DSI}}\right)\subseteq H({\mathring{K}}_{\alpha})
using
Theorem

20.7
combined with the assertion in
Lemma

20.8
which says
{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,k)\leq{\mathring{C}}_{\alpha}{\mathring{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)
. We have
H({\mathring{K}}_{\alpha})\subseteq H({{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha})
using
Theorem

20.7
combined with the fact that
{\mathring{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\leq{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)
.

To show
H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq H({{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha})
, assume
f\in H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
. Then, since
\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\mathrm{d}x=0
, we have that for any
x\in[0,1)

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\widehat{f}}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)

for
w_{k}={\widehat{f}}(k)/{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}(k)
. Then by
Lemma

20.6
it is sufficient to show
(w_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
. To see this, use
Lemma

20.9
to say

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lvert w_{k}\rvert^{2}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\rvert^{2}}{{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptr}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)}\leq{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptC}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{b^{-2{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}}{b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}}={{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptC}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}<\infty

where the last inequality above follows from
[
167
, Remark 19]
.
∎

Theorem 20.13
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
and
x\in[0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\mathring{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\mathring{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptK}\limits^{\vbox to-5.5757pt{\kern-6.02777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\kern 0.79727pt\r{}\kern-3.58748pt\r{}\hss}\vss}}}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the third property in
Lemma

20.2
.
∎

20.3
DSI Kernels for Smooth Functions

Definition 20.14
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
define the RKHS
H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
of real-valued functions
f:[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
with inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{{\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}}=\sum_{l=1}^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(l)}(x)\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{1}g^{(l)}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\alpha)}(x)g^{(\alpha)}(x)\mathrm{d}x

and SPSD kernel defined for any
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
as

{\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{l=1}^{\alpha}\frac{B_{l}(x)B_{l}(x^{\prime})}{(l!)^{2}}+\frac{(-1)^{\alpha+1}}{(2\alpha)!}B_{2\alpha}((x-x^{\prime})\bmod 1)

where
B_{p}
is the
p^{\mathrm{th}}
Bernoulli polynomial.

Remark 20.15
.

H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
is an RKHS of smooth, not necessarily periodic, functions. Moreover,
H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
contains the RKHS of smooth periodic functions
H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
with
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}
given in
Definition

19.1
. Specifically, for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

H\left({\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}\right)=\left\{f\in H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right):\int_{0}^{1}f^{(l)}(x)\mathrm{d}x=0,\quad\forall\;0\leq l<\alpha\right\}.

Lemma 20.16
.

For
\alpha=1
and any
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
we have

{\widecheck{K}}_{1}^{\mathrm{DSI}}(x,x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{6},&x=y\\
\frac{1}{6}-\frac{\lvert{\mathsf{x}}_{\beta}-{\mathsf{y}}_{\beta}\rvert(b-\lvert{\mathsf{x}}_{\beta}-{\mathsf{y}}_{\beta}\rvert)}{b^{\beta+1}},&x\neq y\quad\mathrm{with}\quad\beta=\beta(x\oplus x^{\prime})\end{cases}.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
161
, Appendix B]
.
∎

Corollary 20.17
.

For
\alpha=1
and
b=2
we have

{\widecheck{K}}_{1}^{\mathrm{DSI}}(x,x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{6},&x=y\\
\frac{1}{6}-2^{-1-\beta(x\oplus x^{\prime})},&x\neq y\end{cases}.

Lemma 20.18
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\widecheck{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,0)=\widecheck{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and there is some
{\dot{C}}_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

\widecheck{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,k)\leq{\dot{C}}_{\alpha}b^{-2\mu_{\alpha}(k)}.

Proof.

This is a weaker version of
[
196
, Proposition 20]
which gives explicit bounds on
\lvert\widecheck{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,h)\rvert
for any
k,h\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Lemma 20.19
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
,
f\in H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
, and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there is some
{\ddot{C}}_{\alpha}<\infty
independent of
k
such that

\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\rvert\leq{\ddot{C}}_{\alpha}b^{-\mu_{\alpha}(k)}.

Proof.

[
167
, Theorem 14]
tells us that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\rvert\leq\sum_{l=\#k}^{\alpha}C^{\prime}_{l}b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{l}(k)}+C^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}

(27)

where for
\#k>\alpha
the empty sum
\sum_{l=\#k}^{\alpha}
is defined as
0
and

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{l}

\displaystyle:=\left\lvert\int_{0}^{1}f^{(l)}(x)\mathrm{d}x\right\rvert\frac{1}{\left(2\sin(\pi/b)\right)^{l}}\left(1+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{b(b+1)}\right)^{\max(0,l-2)},

\displaystyle C^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\left\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}x\frac{2}{\left(2\sin(\pi/b)\right)^{\alpha}}\left(1+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{b(b+1)}\right)^{\alpha-2}\left(3+\frac{2}{b}+\frac{2b-1}{b-1}\right).

If
f\in H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
then
\left(\int_{0}^{1}f^{(l)}(x)\mathrm{d}x\right)^{2}<\infty
for
0\leq l\leq\alpha-1
and
\int_{0}^{1}\left(f^{(\alpha)}(x)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x<\infty
. Thus,
C^{\prime}_{l}<\infty
for
0\leq l<\alpha
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}<\infty
follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Let
C_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime\prime}:=\max\left(C^{\prime}_{0},\dots,C^{\prime}_{\alpha-1},C^{\prime}_{\alpha}+C^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha}\right)
so that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
we have

\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}^{\prime}\left[\sum_{l=\#k}^{\alpha-1}b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{l}(k)}+b^{-{\mathring{\mu}}_{\alpha}(k)}\right].

Now, notice that
{\mathring{\mu}}_{l}(k)
is non-decreasing in
l
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, so setting
{\ddot{C}}_{\alpha}=(\alpha+1)C_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and recalling
\mu_{\alpha}(k)={\mathring{\mu}}_{\min(\alpha,\#k)}(k)
yields the result.
∎

Definition 20.20
.

Fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
. We define the weight
{\dot{r}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

{\dot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)=b^{-2\mu_{\alpha}(k)},

and define our SPSD DSI kernel
{\dot{K}}_{\alpha}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{\dot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\dot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime}).

Definition 20.21
.

Fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
. For
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{<1}
, and
\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1}
we define the weight
{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}^{2}(k)=b^{-\mu_{\alpha}(k)-\tau\mu_{1}(k^{\prime})-\zeta\mu_{\alpha-2}(k^{\prime\prime})},

and define our SPSD DSI kernel
K_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime}).

(28)

Theorem 20.22
.

For
\alpha\geq 2
and any
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{<1}
and
\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1}
we have

H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{DSI}}\right)\subseteq H({\dot{K}}_{\alpha})\subseteq H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}\right)

and

H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}\right).

Moreover, for any
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{<1}
and
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1}
satisfying
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{2}
and
\zeta_{1}\geq\zeta_{2}
we have

H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau_{1},\zeta_{1}}\right)\subseteq H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau_{2},\zeta_{2}}\right).

Proof.

We have
H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{DSI}}\right)\subseteq H({\dot{K}}_{\alpha})
using
Theorem

20.7
combined with the assertion in
Lemma

20.18
which says
\widecheck{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\alpha}(k,k)\leq{\dot{C}}_{\alpha}{\dot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)
. We have
H({\dot{K}}_{\alpha})\subseteq H({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta})
using
Theorem

20.7
combined with the fact that
{\dot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\leq{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}^{2}(k)
.

To show
H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}\right)
, assume
f\in H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)
. Then, since

\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\mathrm{d}x=0
, we have that for any
x\in[0,1)

f(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\widehat{f}}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}w_{k}{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)

for
w_{k}={\widehat{f}}(k)/{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(k)
. Then by
Lemma

20.6
it is sufficient to show
(w_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{C}})
. To see this, use
Lemma

20.19
to say

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lvert w_{k}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\lvert{\widehat{f}}(k)\rvert^{2}}{{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}^{2}(k)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{b^{-2\mu_{\alpha}(k)}}{b^{-\mu_{\alpha}(k)-\tau\mu_{1}(k^{\prime})-\zeta\mu_{\alpha-2}(k^{\prime\prime})}}

\displaystyle=C_{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}b^{-\mu_{\alpha}(k)+\tau\mu_{1}(k^{\prime})+\zeta\mu_{\alpha-2}(k^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle<\infty

where the last inequality follows from
[
167
, Remark 19]
.

We have
H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau_{1},\zeta_{1}}\right)\subseteq H\left({\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau_{2},\zeta_{2}}\right)
due to
Theorem

20.7
and the inequality
{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau_{1},\zeta_{1}}\leq{\ddot{r}}_{\alpha,\tau_{2},\zeta_{2}}
.
∎

Remark 20.23
.

[
165
, Corollary 3.11]
provides a much more general result for weighted function spaces
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
. There it is shown that
H\left({\widecheck{K}}_{\alpha}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
for certain parameterizations of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
. Moreover, the error rate of Quasi-Monte Carlo methods for
f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
using higher-order digital nets is optimal.

Definition 20.24
.

The following kernel was studied in
[
161
]
and corresponds to the scramble invariant kernel in
[
199
]
. Fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. We define the weight
{\dddot{r}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
by

{\dddot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)=b^{-(\alpha+1)(\mu_{1}(k)-1)},

and define our SPSD DSI kernel
{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}:[0,1)\times[0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be

{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\dddot{r}}_{\alpha}^{2}(k)\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x\ominus x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\upsilon(\alpha),&x=x^{\prime}\\
\upsilon(\alpha)-b^{-\alpha(\beta(x\ominus x^{\prime})-1)}\left(\upsilon(\alpha)+1\right),&x\neq x^{\prime}\end{cases}

where

\upsilon(\alpha)=\frac{b^{\alpha+1}(b-1)}{b^{\alpha+1}-b}.

Theorem 20.25
.

For any
x\in[0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\dot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\dot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0\quad\forall\;\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 2},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0\quad\forall\;\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 2},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha,\tau,\zeta}(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=0\quad\forall\;\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0},\;\;\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{<1}\;\;\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1}.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the third property in
Lemma

20.2
.
∎

20.4
Computable DSI Kernels in Base 2

This section explores analytical forms of
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}
in base
b=2
.

Definition 20.26
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
and
x\in[0,1)
denote

\omega_{\alpha}(x):=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}b^{-\mu_{\alpha}(k)}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)

(29)

so that for
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
,

{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x,x^{\prime})=\omega_{\alpha}(x\ominus x^{\prime}).

Remark 20.27
.

Explicit expressions of
\omega_{2}(x)
and
\omega_{3}(x)
for any prime
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
are given in
[
162
, Theorem 3]
.

Remark 20.28
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
and prime
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
[
162
, Theorem 2]
shows how to compute
\omega_{\alpha}(x)
in at most
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha\#x)
operations where
x
has at most
\#x
digits in its base
b
representation.

In the following lemma we derive Walsh coefficients of low-order monomials.

Lemma 20.29
.

Fix
b=2
. Let
f_{p}(x):=x^{p}
. When
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
write

k=2^{a_{1}}+\dots+2^{a_{\#k}}

where
a_{1}>a_{2}>\dots>a_{\#k}\geq 0
. Then we have

\displaystyle{\widehat{f}}_{1}(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1/2,&k=0\\
-2^{-a_{1}-2},&k=2^{a_{1}}\\
0,&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases},

\displaystyle{\widehat{f}}_{2}(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1/3,&k=0\\
-2^{-a_{1}-2},&k=2^{a_{1}}\\
2^{-a_{1}-a_{2}-3},&k=2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}\\
0,&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases},

\displaystyle{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1/4,&k=0\\
-2^{-a_{1}-2}+2^{-3a_{1}-5},&k=2^{a_{1}}\\
3\cdot 2^{-a_{1}-a_{2}-4},&k=2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}\\
-3\cdot 2^{-a_{1}-a_{2}-a_{3}-5},&k=2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}\\
0,&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Proof.

The forms for
{\widehat{f}}_{1}
and
{\widehat{f}}_{2}
follow from
[
23
, Example 14.2, Example 14.3]
.
For
k=0
and any
x\in[0,1)
we have
\mathrm{wal}_{0}(x)=1
, so

{\widehat{f}}_{3}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}x^{3}\mathrm{d}x=1/4.

Assume
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
going forward. For
k=2^{a_{1}}+k^{\prime}
with
0\leq k^{\prime}<2^{a_{1}}
,
[
200
, Equation 3.6]
implies

J_{k}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(t)\mathrm{d}t=2^{-a_{1}-2}\left[\mathrm{wal}_{k^{\prime}}(x)-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}2^{-r}\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}+r}+k}(x)\right].

Using integration by parts and the fact that
J_{k}(0)=J_{k}(1)=0
we have

\displaystyle{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}x^{3}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\left[x^{3}J_{k}(x)\right]_{x=0}^{x=1}-3\int_{0}^{1}x^{2}J_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-3*2^{-a_{1}-2}\left[{\widehat{f}}_{2}(k^{\prime})-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}2^{-r}{\widehat{f}}_{2}(2^{a_{1}+r}+k)\right].

If
\#k=1
, i.e.,
k=2^{a_{1}}
, then

\displaystyle{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)

\displaystyle=-3*2^{-a_{1}-2}\left[{\widehat{f}}_{2}(0)-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}2^{-r}{\widehat{f}}_{2}(2^{a_{1}+r}+2^{a_{1}})\right]

\displaystyle=-3*2^{-a_{1}-2}\left[\frac{1}{3}-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}2^{-r}2^{-(a_{1}+r)-a_{1}-3}\right]

\displaystyle=2^{-3a_{1}-5}-2^{-a_{1}-2}.

If
\#k=2
then

{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)=-3*2^{-a_{1}-2}{\widehat{f}}_{2}(2^{a_{2}})=3\cdot 2^{-a_{1}-a_{2}-4}.

If
\#k=3
then

{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)=-3*2^{-a_{1}-2}{\widehat{f}}_{2}(2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}})=-3\cdot 2^{-a_{1}-a_{2}-a_{3}-5}.

If
\#k>3
then
{\widehat{f}}_{3}(k)=0
.
∎

The following theorem gives some explicit forms of
\omega_{\alpha}
in base
b=2
. The
\alpha\in\{2,3\}
forms are due to
[
162
]
. These are also useful in the computation of the worst-case error of QMC with higher-order polynomial-lattice rules as in
[
162
]
.

Theorem 20.30
.

Fix the base
b=2
. Let
\beta(x)=-\lfloor\log_{2}(x)\rfloor
and for
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
define
t_{\nu}(x)=2^{-\nu\beta(x)}
where for
x=0
we set
\beta(x)=t_{\nu}(x)=0
. Then

\displaystyle\omega_{2}(x)=

\displaystyle-1+-\beta(x)x+\frac{5}{2}\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right],

\displaystyle\omega_{3}(x)=

\displaystyle-1+\beta(x)x^{2}-5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x+\frac{43}{18}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right],

\displaystyle\omega_{4}(x)=

\displaystyle-1-\frac{2}{3}\beta(x)x^{3}+5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x^{2}-\frac{43}{9}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right]x

\displaystyle+\frac{701}{294}\left[1-t_{3}(x)\right]+\beta(x)\left[\frac{1}{48}S(x)-\frac{1}{42}\right],

where

S(x)=\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a}}(x)}{2^{3a}}.

(30)

Proof.

Write

\omega_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{1\leq\nu<\alpha}s_{\nu}(x)+{\widetilde{s}}_{\alpha}(x)

where
s_{\nu}
sums over all
k
with
\#k=\nu
and
{\widetilde{s}}_{\alpha}
sums over all
k
with
\#k\geq\alpha
. In
[
162
, Corollary 1]
it was shown that

\displaystyle s_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=-2x+1,

\displaystyle s_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=2x^{2}-2x+\frac{1}{3},

\displaystyle{\widetilde{s}}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\left[2-\beta(x)\right]x+\frac{1}{2}\left[1-5t_{1}(x)\right],

\displaystyle{\widetilde{s}}_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=-\left[2-\beta(x)\right]x^{2}-\left[1-5t_{1}(x)\right]x+\frac{1}{18}\left[1-43t_{2}(x)\right]

from which
\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{3}
follow. We now find expressions for
s_{3}
and
{\widetilde{s}}_{4}
from which
\omega_{4}
follows.

Assume sums over
a_{i}
are over
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
unless otherwise restricted.
Lemma

20.29
gives

\displaystyle x=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+2}},

\displaystyle x^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}-\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+2}}+\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+3}},

\displaystyle x^{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}-\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+2}}+\frac{3}{2}\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}+\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{3a_{1}+5}}

so

x^{3}-\frac{3}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}x=\frac{1}{32}\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{3a_{1}}}-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}

and

s_{3}(x)=\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}=-\frac{4}{3}x^{3}+2x^{2}-\frac{2}{3}x+\frac{1}{24}\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{3a_{1}}}.

Now,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{s}}_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>a_{4}\\
0\leq k<2^{a_{4}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}+2^{a_{4}}+k}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{4}+4}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>a_{4}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}+2^{a_{4}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{4}+4}}\sum_{0\leq k<2^{a_{4}}}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x).

If
x=0
, then

{\widetilde{s}}_{4}(0)=\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>a_{4}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}=\frac{1}{294}.

Going forward, assume
x\in(0,1)
so
\beta(x)=-\lfloor\log_{2}(x)\rfloor
is finite. Recall from the last property of
Lemma

20.2
that

\sum_{0\leq k<2^{a_{4}}}\mathrm{wal}_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}2^{a_{4}},&a_{4}\leq\beta(x)-1\\
0,&a_{4}>\beta(x)-1\end{cases}.

Moreover, since
\beta(x)
is the index of the first
1
in the base
2
expansion of
x
, when
a_{4}<\beta(x)-1
we have
\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{4}}}(x)=(-1)^{{\mathsf{x}}_{a_{4}+1}}=1
and when
a_{4}=\beta(x)-1
we have
\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{4}}}(x)=-1
. This implies

\displaystyle{\widetilde{s}}_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>a_{4}\\
\beta(x)-1\geq a_{4}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}+2^{a_{4}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>a_{4}\\
\beta(x)-1>a_{4}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}-\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}>\beta(x)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}

\displaystyle=:T_{1}-T_{2}.

The first term is

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{4}}\bigg(\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}-\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum_{a_{1}>a_{4}\geq a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}-\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{4}\geq a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{4}}\left[V_{1}(a_{4})-V_{2}(a_{4})-V_{3}(a_{4})-V_{4}(a_{4})\right].

Clearly
V_{1}(a_{4})=s_{3}(x)/2
and
V_{2}
is easily computed. Now

\displaystyle V_{3}(a_{4})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{1}>a_{4}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}\right)\left(s_{1}(x)-\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{1}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+1}}\right)

and

\displaystyle V_{4}(a_{4})=\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{3}+2}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{4}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{3}+2}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}-\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}-\sum_{a_{1}>a_{4}\geq a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{3}+2}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(s_{2}(x)-\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}-\left(s_{1}(x)-\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{1}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+1}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{4}\geq a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+1}}\right)\right).

The second term is

\displaystyle T_{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}+2^{a_{3}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{1}>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{a_{1}>\beta(x)-1>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}+\sum_{a_{1}>\beta(x)-1>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+\beta(x)+a_{3}+3}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>\beta(x)-1>a_{3}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+4}}+\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>\beta(x)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+\beta(x)+3}}

\displaystyle=:W_{1}-W_{2}+W_{3}-W_{4}+W_{5}-W_{6}+W_{7}.

Clearly
W_{1}=s_{3}(x)/2
and both
W_{2}
and
W_{3}
are easily computed. Similarity in the next two sums gives

\displaystyle W_{5}

\displaystyle-W_{4}=\left(\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+a_{3}+2}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{1}>\beta(x)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+a_{3}+2}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+a_{3}+3}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(s_{1}(x)-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{1}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)}}\right)

Similarity in the final two sums gives

\displaystyle W_{7}-W_{6}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+1}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{3}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{3}+2}}\right)\left(\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>\beta(x)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}\right)

where

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}>\beta(x)-1}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}=\sum_{a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}+2^{a_{2}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+a_{2}+1}}-\sum_{a_{1}>\beta(x)-1>a_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}+\sum_{a_{1}>\beta(x)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{a_{1}+\beta(x)+1}}

\displaystyle=s_{2}(x)-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{1}>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+a_{2}+2}}+\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)+a_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)}}-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{2}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{2}+1}}\right)\left(s_{1}(x)-\sum_{\beta(x)-1>a_{1}}\frac{1}{2^{a_{1}+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{\beta(x)}}\right).

This implies

\displaystyle{\widetilde{s}}_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\left(2-\beta(x)\right)x^{3}+\left(1-5\ t_{1}(x)\right)x^{2}-\frac{1}{9}\left(1-43t_{2}(x)\right)x

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{48}\left(2-\beta(x)\right)\sum_{a_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a_{1}}}(x)}{2^{3a_{1}}}-\frac{1}{294}\left(7\beta(x)+701t_{3}(x)\right)+\frac{5}{98},

from which the result follows.
∎

20.5
Derivatives of DSI Kernels in Base 2

For
b=2
, let
{\mathbb{B}}
denote the set of all dyadic rationals, i.e.,
x\in[0,1)
whose binary expansion has a finite number of ones. For
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
let
\Omega(x)
be the index of the last
1
in that binary expansion of
x
, e.g., if
x=3/4=0.11_{2}
then
\Omega(x)=2
.

Lemma 20.31
.

For base
b=2
, any dyadic rational
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
, and
S
defined in (
30
), the partial derivatives satisfy

\partial^{+}S(x)=0\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\partial^{-}S(x)=-\infty.

(31)

Proof.

Let
\Omega=\Omega(x)
and recall
\beta(x)=-\lfloor\log_{2}(x)\rfloor
is the index of the first
1
in the binary expansion of
x
. For
h<2^{-\Omega}
we have

S(x+h)-S(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq\Omega}\left[\frac{(-1)^{{\mathsf{h}}_{\ell+1}}}{2^{3\ell}}-\frac{1}{2^{3\ell}}\right].

(32)

Clearly
\partial^{+}S(x)\leq 0
. However,

\displaystyle\partial^{+}S(x)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{h}\sum_{\ell\geq\beta(h)-1}\left[\frac{-1}{2^{3\ell}}-\frac{-1}{2^{3\ell}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{h}\sum_{\ell\geq\beta(h)-1}\frac{1}{2^{3\ell-1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{7}}{7}\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-3\beta(h)}}{h}

\displaystyle=0,

so
\partial^{+}S(x)=0
.

The left derivative, assuming
x\in{\mathbb{B}}\setminus\{0\}
, is

\displaystyle\partial^{-}S(x)=

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{S(x)-S(x-h)}{h}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{h}\bigg[\bigg(-\frac{1}{2^{3(\Omega-1)}}+\sum_{\ell\geq\Omega}\frac{1}{2^{3\ell}}\bigg)-\bigg(\frac{1}{2^{3(\Omega-1)}}-\sum_{\ell\geq\Omega}\frac{1}{2^{3\ell}}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{h}\left(\sum_{\ell\geq\Omega}\frac{1}{2^{3\ell-1}}-\frac{1}{2^{3\Omega-4}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{7}2^{5-3\Omega}\left(\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{h}\right)

\displaystyle=-\infty.

∎

Lemma 20.32
.

For base
b=2
and any dyadic rational
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
we have

\partial^{+}\omega_{4}(x)=-2(1+\omega_{3}(x))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial^{+}\omega_{3}(x)=-2(1+\omega_{2}(x)).

Proof.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{B}}\setminus\{0\}
we can choose
h>0
small enough so that
\beta(x)=\beta(x+h)
. Combined with
Lemma

20.31
, this implies
\omega_{4}
and
\omega_{3}
are both right differentiable on
{\mathbb{B}}\setminus\{0\}
. The fact that
\partial^{+}\omega_{3}(0)=-5
follows from

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\omega_{3}(h)-\omega_{3}(0)}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)h^{2}-5\left(1-t_{1}(h)\right)h+\frac{43}{18}\left(1-t_{2}(h)\right)-\frac{43}{18}}{h}.

The fact that
\partial^{+}\omega_{4}(0)=-43/9
follows from

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\omega_{4}(h)-\omega_{4}(0)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{-\frac{2}{3}\beta(h)h^{3}+5\left(1-t_{1}(h)\right)x^{2}-\frac{43}{9}\left(1-t_{2}(h)\right)h+\frac{701}{294}\left(1-t_{3}(h)\right)-\frac{701}{294}}{h}

and

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h}\left(\frac{1}{48}S(x)-\frac{1}{42}\right)=0

where
S
is given in (
30
). The above equation follows from writing

\displaystyle\frac{1}{48}S(x)-\frac{1}{42}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{48}\left(\sum_{\beta(h)-1>a}\frac{1}{2^{3a}}-\frac{1}{2^{3(\beta(h)-1)}}+\sum_{a>\beta(h)-1}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a}}(h)}{2^{3a}}\right)-\frac{1}{42}

\displaystyle\in\left[-\frac{8}{21}2^{-3\beta(h)},-\frac{1}{3}2^{-3\beta(h)}\right]

so that

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h}\left(\frac{1}{48}S(x)-\frac{1}{42}\right)\geq-\frac{8}{21}\left(\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h2^{3\beta(h)}}\right)=0

and

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h}\left(\frac{1}{48}S(x)-\frac{1}{42}\right)\leq-\frac{1}{3}\left(\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h2^{3\beta(h)}}\right)=0.

∎

Lemma 20.33
.

For base
b=2
and dyadic rationals
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{B}}
, the right derivatives satisfy

\partial_{x}^{+}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{+}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=1,

while the left derivatives satisfy

\partial_{x}^{-}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{-}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\infty.

Proof.

Let
\Omega=\max\{\Omega(x),\Omega(x^{\prime})\}
. For
h<2^{-\Omega}
we have
(x+h)\oplus x^{\prime}=(x\oplus x^{\prime})+h
so the right partial derivative satisfies

\partial_{x}^{+}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{((x+h)\oplus x^{\prime})-(x\oplus x^{\prime})}{h}=1.

(33)

However, for
h<2^{-\Omega}
we have
(x-h)\oplus x^{\prime}\geq 2^{-a(x)}
so the left partial derivative is
\partial_{x}^{-}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\infty
. The same arguments can be used to show
\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{+}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=1
while
\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{-}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\infty
.
∎

Corollary 20.34
.

Fix the base
b=2
. For
\alpha\in\{3,4\}
and dyadic rationals
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{B}}
the right derivative satisfies

\partial_{x}^{+}{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x,x^{\prime})=\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{+}{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x,x^{\prime})=-2\left(1+{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha-1,0,0}(x,x^{\prime})\right).

Proof.

This follows immediately from combining
Lemma

20.32
and
Lemma

20.33
.
∎

Theorem 20.35
.

Fix the base
b=2
. For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and any dyadic rationals
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{B}}
the right derivative satisfies

\partial_{x}^{+}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{+}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=0.

Proof.

For
x\neq x^{\prime}
the claim is obvious since
\beta((x+h)\oplus x^{\prime})=\beta(x\oplus x^{\prime})=\beta(x\oplus(x^{\prime}+h))
for
h\in(0,1)
small enough. When
x=x^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h,x)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(h,0)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(0,0)}{h}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{2^{2\alpha+1}}{2^{\alpha+1}-2}+2^{\alpha}\right)\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h)}}{h}.

Now, using the fact that
2^{-\beta(h)}\leq h\leq 2^{1-\beta(h)}
whenever
h\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle 0=2^{-\alpha}\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{h^{\alpha}}{h}\leq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h)}}{h}\leq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{h^{\alpha}}{h}=0,

so
\partial_{x}^{+}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=0
. Swapping
x
and
x^{\prime}
in the above statements gives the proof for
\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{+}{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=0
.
∎

Theorem 20.36
.

For base
b=2
and any dyadic rational
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
, the right limits satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\infty,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\infty,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\infty.

Proof.

For
\alpha=2
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(x\oplus x,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}((x+h)\oplus x,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(0,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{2,0,0}(h,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)h+\frac{5}{2}2^{-\beta(h)}}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\beta(h)}{h}

\displaystyle=\infty

For
\alpha=3
, notice that
2^{-\beta(h)}\leq h
whenever
h\in(0,1)
, so

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(x\oplus x,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}((x+h)\oplus x,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(0,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{3,0,0}(h,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{-\beta(h)h^{2}+5\left[1-2^{-\beta(h)}\right]h+\frac{43}{18}\cdot 2^{-2\beta(h)}}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left(5\left[1-2^{-\beta(h)}\right]h^{-1}-\beta(h)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left(5\left[1-h\right]h^{-1}-\beta(h)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left(5h^{-1}+\lfloor\log_{2}(h)\rfloor-5\right)

\displaystyle=\infty

For
\alpha=4
, notice
S(h)/48\leq 1/42
so
\beta(h)\left[\frac{1}{48}S(h)-\frac{1}{42}\right]<0
where
S
is given in (
30
). Now, using the fact that
h/2\leq 2^{-\beta(h)}\leq h
whenever
h\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(x,x)-{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(x\oplus x,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}((x+h)\oplus x,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(0,0)-{\ddot{K}}_{4,0,0}(h,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\frac{2}{3}\beta(h)h^{3}-5\left[1-2^{-\beta(h)}\right]h^{2}+\frac{43}{9}\left[1-2^{-2\beta(h)}\right]h+\frac{701}{294}\left[2^{-3\beta(h)}\right]}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{-5\left[1-2^{-\beta(h)}\right]h^{2}+\frac{43}{9}\left[1-2^{-2\beta(h)}\right]h+\frac{701}{294}\left[2^{-3\beta(h)}\right]}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left(\frac{43}{9}\left[1-2^{-2\beta(h)}\right]h^{-1}-5\left[1-2^{-\beta(h)}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left(\frac{43}{9}\left[1-h^{2}\right]h^{-1}-5\left[1-h/2\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\infty

∎

Theorem 20.37
.

Fix the base
b=2
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and any dyadic rational
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
, the right limit satisfies

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\begin{cases}\infty,&\alpha\in(0,2)\\
\text{does not exist},&\alpha=2\\
0,&\alpha>2\end{cases}.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>2}
, the right limit satisfies

\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0+}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{2},x)+{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}=0.

Proof.

For the first claim,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h,x)}{h^{2}}=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x\oplus x,0)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}((x+h)\oplus x,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(0,0)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(h,0)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2^{2\alpha+1}}{2^{\alpha+1}-2}+2^{\alpha}\right)\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h)}}{h^{2}}.

Recalling that
h/2\leq 2^{-\beta(h)}\leq h
whenever
h\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h)}}{h^{2}}\in\left[2^{-\alpha}\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{h^{\alpha}}{h^{2}},\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{h^{\alpha}}{h^{2}}\right]=\left[2^{-\alpha}\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}h^{\alpha-2},\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}h^{\alpha-2}\right].

This proves the
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\alpha>2
claims. To see the
\alpha=2
claim, take two subsequences
(h_{p})_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}:=(1/2^{p})_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(w_{p})_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}=(1/2^{p}+1/2^{p+1})_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}
which are both monotonically decreasing to
0
as
p\to\infty
. Then

\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{2^{-2\beta(h_{p})}}{h_{p}^{2}}=\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{2^{-2p}}{2^{-2p}}=1\qquad\mathrm{but}\qquad\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{2^{-2\beta(w_{p})}}{w_{p}^{2}}=\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{2^{-2p}}{(2^{-p}+2^{-p-1})^{2}}=\frac{4}{9},

so the limit does not exist.

For the second claim, with
\alpha>2
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0+}

\displaystyle\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{1},x)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x+h_{2},x)+{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0+}\frac{{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{1},0)-{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(h_{2},0)+{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(0,0)}{h_{1}h_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2^{2\alpha+1}}{2^{\alpha+1}-2}+2^{\alpha}\right)\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0^{+}}\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{1})}+2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{2})}-2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})}}{h_{1}h_{2}}.

Let us denote

L:=\frac{2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{1})}+2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{2})}-2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})}}{h_{1}h_{2}}.

Suppose
h_{1},h_{2}\in(0,1)
and
\beta(h_{1})=p
and
\beta(h_{2})=q
, i.e.,
h_{1}=2^{-p}+\cdots
and
h_{2}=2^{-q}+\cdots
. Notice that
\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})\geq\min(p,q)
so
(1-\alpha)\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})\leq(1-\alpha)\min(p,q)
and
2^{(1-\alpha)\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})}\leq 2^{(1-\alpha)\min(p,q)}
. Therefore,

2^{-\alpha p}+2^{-\alpha q}-2^{-\alpha\beta(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})}\geq 2^{-\alpha p}+2^{(1-\alpha)q}-2^{-\alpha\min(p,q)}\geq 0,

so
L\geq 0
.
We know
2^{-p}\leq h_{1}\leq 2^{-p+1}
and
2^{-q}\leq h_{2}\leq 2^{-q+1}
. Let
\alpha=2+\kappa
for some
\kappa>0
. Then

1.

If
p=q
, then

L\leq\frac{2^{-\alpha p}+2^{-\alpha p}}{2^{-p}2^{-p}}=2^{(2-\alpha)p+1}=2^{1-\kappa p},

so
1-\kappa p\leq-\tau
whenever
q=p\geq(1+\tau)/\kappa
, which ensures
L\leq 2^{-\tau}

2.

If
p>q
, then

L\leq\frac{2^{-\alpha p}+2^{-\alpha q}-2^{-\alpha q}}{2^{-p}2^{-q}}=2^{(1-\alpha)p+q}\leq 2^{(2-\alpha)q}=2^{-\kappa q},

so
-\kappa q\leq-\tau
whenever
p>q\geq\tau/\kappa
, which ensures
L\leq 2^{-\tau}
.

3.

If
q>p
, then

L\leq\frac{2^{-\alpha p}+2^{-\alpha q}-2^{-\alpha p}}{2^{-p}2^{-q}}=2^{(1-\alpha)q+p}\leq 2^{(2-\alpha)p}=2^{-\kappa p},

so
-\kappa p\leq-\tau
whenever
q>p\geq\tau/\kappa
, which ensures
L\leq 2^{-\tau}
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\tau\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that
2^{-\tau}<\varepsilon
. For
\delta=2^{-(1+\tau)/\kappa}
, if
\sqrt{h_{1}^{2}+h_{2}^{2}}\leq\delta
then
0<h_{1}\leq\delta
and
0<h_{2}\leq\delta
which ensures
p\geq(1+\tau)/\kappa
and
q\geq(1+\tau)/\kappa
. Then all three of the above cases are satisfied which ensures
0\leq L\leq 2^{-\tau}<\varepsilon
.
∎

\Chapter

Gaussian Processes

This section will discuss Gaussian process (GP) regression. We will begin with standard GPs in
Section

21
and then review some popular kernel forms in
Section

22
, including summaries of the shift-invariant (SI) kernels from
Section

19
and digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernels from
Section

20
. Then,
Section

23
will briefly mention a few connections between RKHS kernel interpolation and GP regression. Next,
Section

24
will discuss fast GPs using lattices with SI kernels or digital nets with DSI kernels using the fast kernel computations of
Section

15.4.3
. After discussing standard GPs and fast GPs,
Section

25
and
Section

26
will detail multitask GPs and fast multitask GPs respectively with the latter constituting our novel contribution to the GP literature.
Section

27
will discuss (fast) derivative-informed GPs as a special case of (fast) multitask GPs. Finally,
Section

28
will present some numerical experiments using our
FastGPs
package.

21
Standard GPs

Here we present some notation and background for standard GP regression in
Section

21.1
and then discuss kernel hyperparameter optimization in
Section

21.2
. Standard GP regression and hyperparameter optimization has been thoroughly studied, see the cornerstone work
[
51
]
or
[
52
,
201
,
202
,
203
]
.

21.1
Background

Let us assume
f
is a GP on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with prior mean
M:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a SPSD (covariance) kernel
K:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
(see
Definition

16.1
):

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{GP}(M,K),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})]

\displaystyle=M({\boldsymbol{x}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[f({\boldsymbol{x}}),f({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}).

The fast GPs we present use
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1)^{d}
, and we will always assume evaluating the kernel
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})
costs
{\mathcal{O}}(d)
. The parameterization of the prior mean
M
may be quite flexible, e.g., a neural network.

Suppose we have observed

{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{f}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\qquad\boldsymbol{f}=(f({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{n-1},\qquad({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}\subset{\mathcal{X}},\qquad{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\boldsymbol{0}},\xi{\mathsf{I}}).

(34)

Here
{\boldsymbol{y}}
are noisy function evaluations at collocation points
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
, and the zero-mean IID Gaussian noise
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
has common noise variance (nugget)
\xi>0
.

Proposition 21.1
.

For arbitrary
T_{1},T_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{1}}
and
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{2}}
are Gaussian with

\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\\
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}\\
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{C}}_{11}&{\mathsf{C}}_{12}\\
{\mathsf{C}}_{12}^{\intercal}&{\mathsf{C}}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right)

where
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{1}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{2}}
and
{\mathsf{C}}_{11}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{1}\times T_{1}},{\mathsf{C}}_{12}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{1}\times T_{2}},{\mathsf{C}}_{22}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T_{2}\times T_{2}}
. Then

{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}\mid{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}+{\mathsf{C}}_{12}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{C}}_{11}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}),{\mathsf{C}}_{22}-{\mathsf{C}}_{12}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{C}}_{11}^{-1}{\mathsf{C}}_{12}\right).

Proof.

See
[
51
, Appendix A.2]
.
∎

The posterior distribution of
f
given noisy observations
{\boldsymbol{y}}
is also a Gaussian process with closed form posterior mean and variance:

\displaystyle f|{\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{GP}({\mathbb{E}}[f|{\boldsymbol{y}}],\mathrm{Cov}[f,f|{\boldsymbol{y}}]),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle=M({\boldsymbol{x}})+{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{M}}),

(35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[f({\boldsymbol{x}}),f({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle=K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}).

(36)

Here
{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}}):=(K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{n-1}
is a vector of kernel evaluations,
{\boldsymbol{M}}=(M({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{n-1}
is a vector of prior mean evaluations, and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
is the noisy version of the Gram matrix, i.e.,

{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}={\mathsf{K}}+\xi{\mathsf{I}},\qquad{\mathsf{K}}:=(K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i^{\prime}}))_{i,i^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}.

Notice the posterior covariance (
36
) only depends on the sampling locations
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
and kernel
K
, not the prior mean
M
or noisy evaluations
{\boldsymbol{y}}
. We will always assume a positive noise variance,
\xi>0
, so
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
is always a SPD matrix (see
Definition

16.2
). In practice, if observations are noise free we still set the nugget
\xi
to a small positive number for numerical stability, e.g.,
\xi=10^{-8}
.

The computational bottleneck for GPs is the requirement to solve the linear systems in the noisy Gram matrix
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
which appear in the equations for the posterior mean (
35
) and posterior covariance (
36
). Additionally, one may need to evaluate the determinant
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert
if performing kernel hyperparameter optimization as we discuss in the next section. To solve a linear system in
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
, standard practice is to compute the Cholesky decomposition, which costs
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
computations and
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2})
storage, and then perform back-substitution solves at cost
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2})
. Inference of the posterior mean (
35
) and posterior covariance (
36
) can then be performed at
{\mathcal{O}}(n)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2})
cost respectively.
Table

6
summarizes these costs and compares against the fast GP methods we will present in
Section

24
.

21.2
Hyperparameter Optimization (HPOPT)

The kernel
K
typically relies on hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, e.g.,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{\gamma,{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\}
for a global scale
\gamma
and lengthscales
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, or
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\gamma,{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\}
to additionally optimize smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. We consider two loss functions below: the negative marginal log-likelihood (NMLL) and the generalized cross validation (GCV). Analysis of the NMLL and GCV losses was performed in
[
72
]
for both standard GPs and the fast GP variants we will present in
Section

24
.
[
72
]
also analyzes a full Bayesian loss which we do not consider here.

21.3
HPOPT: Negative Marginal Log-Likelihood

Perhaps the most popular optimization objective for GPs is the NMLL, which, up to an additive constant independent of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, is proportional to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{NMLL}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=({\boldsymbol{y}}-M({\boldsymbol{x}}))^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{y}}-M({\boldsymbol{x}}))+\log\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert.

(37)

Here we have hidden the implicit dependence of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
on kernel hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. If the prior mean is a constant
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e.,
M({\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau
for all
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\tau
may be treated as an additional hyperparameter. The NMLL loss is then proportional to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{NMLL}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=({\boldsymbol{y}}-\tau{\boldsymbol{1}})^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{y}}-\tau{\boldsymbol{1}})+\log\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert.

Setting the derivative with respect to
\tau
equal to
0
gives the optimal constant prior mean

{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}}{{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{1}}}.

21.4
HPOPT: Generalized Cross Validation

The generalized cross validation loss
[
201
,
202
,
203
]
is proportional to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GCV}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=\frac{({\boldsymbol{y}}-M({\boldsymbol{x}}))^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}({\boldsymbol{y}}-M({\boldsymbol{x}}))}{\mathrm{trace}^{2}({\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1})}

(38)

where again we have hidden the implicit dependence of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
on kernel hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. If the prior mean is a constant
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e.,
M({\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau
for all
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, the GCV loss becomes proportional to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GCV}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=\frac{({\boldsymbol{y}}-\tau{\boldsymbol{1}})^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}({\boldsymbol{y}}-\tau{\boldsymbol{1}})}{\mathrm{trace}^{2}\left({\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}\right)}.

Setting the derivative with respect to
\tau
equal to
0
gives the optimal prior mean constant

{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}{\boldsymbol{y}}}{{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}{\boldsymbol{1}}}.

22
Kernels

This section will review a number of available SPD kernels. This is by no means an exhaustive list, see
[
51
, Chapter 4]
,
[
52
]
, or
[
195
, Chapter 2]
for additional details. We will begin by collecting results from
Section

19
on shift-invariant (SI) kernels and
Section

20
on digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernels. We will only present product-form SI and DSI kernels (
25
). Mixture of product SI and DSI kernels (
24
) are also theoretically compatible in the fast GPs framework we will present in
Section

24
, but their evaluation cost is exponential in the dimension and therefore impractical in most applications. After presenting product-form SI and DSI kernels, we will review some popular kernels including the squared exponential (SE) kernel, the Matérn kernels, and the rational quadratic kernel.
Figure

17
visualizes a few draws from a GP prior when using the popular SE kernel in
Definition

22.3
, a SI kernel from
Definition

22.1
, or a DSI kernel from
Definition

22.2
.

Figure 17
:
Prior draws from
1
-dimensional GPs having a squared exponential (SE) kernel with
\eta=10^{-2}
, a shift-invariant (SI) kernel with
\alpha=2
and
\eta=1
(see
Section

17.1
or
Definition

22.1
built from univariate
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}
), and a digitally-SI (DSI) kernel with
\alpha=4
and
\eta=1
(see
Section

17.2
or
Definition

22.2
built form univariate
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}
). The global scaling parameter
\gamma
is chosen so all draws have similar ranges. Note the SI draws are periodic and the DSI draws are discontinuous.

22.1
Summary of Shift-Invariant (SI) Kernels

Definition 22.1
.

The shift-invariant (SI) product kernels studied in
Section

19
take the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(1+\eta_{j}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right),\qquad\forall\;{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}

where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}^{d}
are smoothness parameters. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
satisfying
2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime}>1
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:={\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha}^{(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}((x-x^{\prime})\bmod 1,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{\alpha+\beta^{\prime}+1}(2\pi)^{2\alpha}}{(2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime})!}B_{2\alpha-\beta-\beta^{\prime}}((x-x^{\prime})\bmod 1)

where
B_{p}
is the
p^{\mathrm{th}}
Bernoulli polynomial. Explicitly,

B_{p}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{p}\binom{p}{j}{\widetilde{B}}_{p-j}x^{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{p}c_{p,j}x^{j}

(39)

where
{\widetilde{B}}_{j}
is the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
Bernoulli numbers. Some
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{p}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}
for small
p
are given in
Table

7
.

Table 7
:
Coefficients
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{p}
for the Bernoulli polynomial
B_{p}
defined in (
39
).

0

1

For
{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
satisfying
2{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime}>1
elementwise, we have

\displaystyle K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}+\eta_{j}K^{(\beta_{j},\beta_{j}^{\prime})}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1)^{d}}\int_{[0,1)^{d}}K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\gamma

for all
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1)^{d}
where
1_{\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0}
is
1
if
\beta_{j}+\beta_{j}^{\prime}=0
and
0
otherwise. In
Section

27
we will see that, for
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}>3/2
elementwise, GPs using these SI kernels are mean squared differentiable.

22.2
Summary of Digitally-Shift-Invariant (DSI) Kernels

Definition 22.2
.

The digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) product kernels studied in
Section

20
take the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(1+\eta_{j}K_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime})\right),\qquad\forall\;{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1)^{d}

where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
are smoothness parameters. Here we only list results for the base
b=2
kernels, although for most results
Section

20
has analogs for arbitrary prime bases. We have at least two computable DSI kernel options which are defined using
\beta(x):=-\lfloor\log_{2}(x)\rfloor
and
x\oplus x^{\prime}:=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}(({\mathsf{x}}_{k}+{\mathsf{x}}_{k}^{\prime})\bmod 2)2^{-k}
, the XOR of binary expansions
x=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathsf{x}}_{k}2^{-k}
and
x^{\prime}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathsf{x}}_{k}^{\prime}2^{-k}
, with
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1)
.

1.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
,

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:={\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x\oplus x^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x,0)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-1+-\beta(x)x+\frac{5}{2}\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right],&\alpha=2\\
-1+\beta(x)x^{2}-5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x+\frac{43}{18}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right],&\alpha=3\\
-1-\frac{2}{3}\beta(x)x^{3}+5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x^{2}-\frac{43}{9}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right]x\\
\quad+\frac{701}{294}\left[1-t_{3}(x)\right]+\beta(x)\left[\frac{1}{48}\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{wal}_{2^{a}}(x)}{2^{3a}}-\frac{1}{42}\right],&\alpha=4\end{cases}

where for
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
we use
t_{\nu}(x)=2^{-\nu\beta(x)}
. Note that for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
one may compute this
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}(x,x^{\prime})
at
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha\#(x\oplus x^{\prime}))
costs using
[
162
, Theorem 2]
where
x\oplus x^{\prime}
has at most
\#(x\oplus x^{\prime})
digits in its base
b
representation. The
\alpha\in\{3,4\}
kernels are right differentiable everywhere on
[0,1)^{d}
, see
Theorem

20.36
, but GPs which use these kernels are not mean squared differentiable as we will show in
Section

27
.

2.

For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime}):={\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}(x,x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\upsilon(\alpha),&x=x^{\prime}\\
\upsilon(\alpha)-2^{-\alpha(\beta(x\oplus x^{\prime})-1)}\left(\upsilon(\alpha)+1\right),&x\neq x^{\prime}\end{cases}

with
\upsilon(\alpha)=2^{\alpha+1}/(2^{\alpha+1}-2)
. These kernels have right derivative
0
everywhere, see
Theorem

20.35
, and for
\alpha>2
they are mean squared differentiable as we will show in
Section

27
.

For either of these DSI kernels,

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{d}}K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1)^{d}}\int_{[0,1)^{d}}K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\gamma

for all
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1)^{d}
as was the case for the SI kernels summarized in
Definition

22.1
.

22.3
Popular Radially Symmetric Kernels

Throughout the remainder of this section we will denote the scaled distance by

\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{(x_{j}-x_{j}^{\prime})^{2}}{2\eta_{j}^{2}}}

where
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{d}
are lengthscales. Radially symmetric kernels are kernels which are only a function of the scaled distance between locations, i.e.,
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=Q(\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
for some
Q:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
. We define a few of the more popular radially symmetric kernels below, see
[
52
, Appendix D]
for a more in depth discussion. A global scale
\gamma>0
will also be considered.

Definition 22.3
.

The squared exponential (SE) kernel, also called the Gaussian kernel, takes the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\exp\left(-\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{2}\right).

This may also be written as a product of one-dimensional kernels. For any
x\in[0,1]^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}}K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{d/2}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\sqrt{\eta_{j}}\left(\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2\eta_{j}}}\right)-\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{2\eta_{j}}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left[2\eta_{j}^{2}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\eta_{j}^{2}}\right)-1\right)+\sqrt{2\pi}\eta_{j}\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\eta_{j}}\right)\right]

where
\mathrm{erf}(x)=2/\sqrt{\pi}\int_{0}^{x}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t
is the error function.

Definition 22.4
.

The Matérn kernel with smoothness parameter
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1/2}
is

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\frac{2^{1-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\left(\sqrt{2\alpha}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right)^{\alpha}K_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{2\alpha}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right)

where
\Gamma
is the gamma function and, in this definition only,
K_{\alpha}
denotes the modified Bessel function. As
\alpha\to\infty
the Matérn kernel converges to the squared exponential kernel in
Definition

22.3
. For
\alpha=p+1/2
with
p\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\gamma\frac{p!}{(2p)!}e^{-\sqrt{2p+1}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\sum_{i=0}^{p}\frac{(p+i)!}{i!(p-i)!}\left(2\sqrt{2p+1}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right)^{p-i}

\displaystyle=\gamma\exp\left(-\sqrt{2\alpha}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\right)\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha-1/2}c_{\alpha,j}\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{j}

(40)

where
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{\alpha+1/2}
are some coefficients. Some
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\alpha}
for small
\alpha
are given in
Table

8
.

Table 8
:
Coefficients
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\alpha}
for the Matérn kernel when
\alpha=p+1/2
with
p\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
in the form of (
40
).

\alpha/j
