Definition 22.5
.

The rational quadratic kernel with scale mixture parameter
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
takes the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\left(1+\frac{\lVert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}{\alpha}\right)^{-\alpha}

As
\alpha\to\infty
the rational quadratic kernel converges to the squared exponential kernel in
Definition

22.3
.

23
Connections Between GPs and RKHS Kernel Interpolation

In this section we review a few known connections between GP regression and RKHS kernel interpolation. Details on RKHS theory are available in
[
53
]
. Additional connections between GPs and RKHSs are discussed in
[
55
,
54
]
.

23.1
Approximation

Theorem 23.1
.

Let
H
be an RKHS with kernel
K
. Suppose we are given an arbitrary error function
E:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a regularization function
R:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

\exists\;{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\qquad\text{such that}\qquad{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}(\cdot)\in\argmin_{f\in H}\left[E\left(\boldsymbol{f}\right)+R\left(\lVert f\rVert_{H}\right)\right]

where
\boldsymbol{f}=(f({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{n-1}
are function evaluations at some points
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}\subset{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

For any
f\in H
define the orthogonal decomposition

f={\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}+\nu

where
\nu({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})=\left\langle\nu,K(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\right\rangle_{H}=0
for
0\leq i<n
. To see
E\left(\boldsymbol{f}\right)
is independent of
\nu
, notice that

f({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})=\left\langle f,K(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\right\rangle_{H}=\left\langle{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}+\nu,K(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\right\rangle_{H}=\left\langle{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal},{\boldsymbol{K}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\right\rangle_{H}

for
0\leq i<n
. To see
R\left(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H}\right)
attains its minimum when
\nu=0
, recall
R
is non-decreasing and

R(\lVert f\rVert_{H})=R\left(\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}+\nu\right\rVert_{H}\right)=R\left(\sqrt{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}+\lVert\nu\rVert_{H}^{2}}\right).

∎

Theorem 23.2
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
are given. Then
f\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined as

f(\cdot)\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}:={\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}(\cdot){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}

(41)

attains the minimum of

\left({\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{f}\right)^{\intercal}\left({\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{f}\right)+\xi^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{H}^{2}

(42)

among all
f\in H
.

Proof.

Theorem

23.1
implies a minimizer takes the form

f\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{c}}

(43)

for some
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Since
\lVert f\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}\rVert_{H}^{2}={\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{c}}
, plugging
f\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}
into (
42
) gives

\displaystyle L({\boldsymbol{c}})

\displaystyle:=\left({\boldsymbol{y}}-{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{c}}\right)^{\intercal}\left({\boldsymbol{y}}-{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{c}}\right)+\xi^{2}{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{c}}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{y}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{y}}-2{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{y}}+{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{K}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\boldsymbol{c}}.

L({\boldsymbol{c}})
is minimized where the gradient is
{\boldsymbol{0}}
which occurs when

\displaystyle{\mathsf{K}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\boldsymbol{c}}={\mathsf{K}}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

Since
L({\boldsymbol{c}})
is convex in
{\boldsymbol{c}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
is non-singular, a minimizer of
L({\boldsymbol{c}})
is

{\boldsymbol{c}}={\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

Plugging this
{\boldsymbol{c}}
into (
43
) yields (
41
).
∎

Corollary 23.3
.

The optimal RKHS kernel interpolant in
Theorem

23.2
is equivalent to the posterior mean (
35
) of a GP with zero prior mean, i.e.,

f\nmid{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathbb{E}}[f\mid{\boldsymbol{y}}]\qquad\text{subject to}\qquad M({\boldsymbol{x}})=0,\qquad\forall\;{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}.

23.2
Point-wise Error

Let us define the noisy kernel

{\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})+\xi 1_{{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}

(44)

where
1_{{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}}
is
1
if
{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}
and
0
otherwise.

Lemma 23.4
.

For any RKHS
H
and any
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we have

\sup_{f\in H:\lVert f\rVert_{H}\leq 1}{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{f}=\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}\right\rVert_{H}

Proof.

The reproducing property and the Cauchy–Schwartz inequality imply

\sup_{f\in H:\lVert f\rVert_{H}\leq 1}{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{f}=\sup_{f\in H:\lVert f\rVert_{H}\leq 1}\left\langle f,{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}\right\rangle_{H}\leq\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}\right\rVert_{H}.

Let
g={\boldsymbol{c}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{K}}
. Then
g/\lVert g\rVert_{H}\in H
has
H
-norm
1
and attains the above upper bound.
∎

Theorem 23.5
.

Using the notations
H:=H(K)
and
{\widetilde{H}}:=H({\widetilde{K}})
, we have that for any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
,

\sup_{{\widetilde{f}}\in{\widetilde{H}}:\lVert{\widetilde{f}}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}}\leq 1}\left\lvert{\widetilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}\right\rvert^{2}={\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-2\xi f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\boldsymbol{1}}({\boldsymbol{x}})

where
{\boldsymbol{1}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=(1_{{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}})_{i=0}^{n-1}
.

Proof.

From
Theorem

23.2
,
Lemma

23.4
, and using the definition of the noisy kernel
{\widetilde{K}}
in (
44
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in{\widetilde{H}}:\lVert{\widetilde{f}}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}}\leq 1}

\displaystyle\left({\widetilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}\right)=\sup_{f\in{\widetilde{H}}:\lVert{\widetilde{f}}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}}\leq 1}\left({\widetilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert{\widetilde{K}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{K}}}\right\rVert_{{\widetilde{H}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-2{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{K}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})+{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-2\xi{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{1}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-2\xi f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\boldsymbol{1}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}.

We may take care of the absolute value by noting the symmetry

\sup_{{\widetilde{f}}\in{\widetilde{H}}:\lVert{\widetilde{f}}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}}\leq 1}\left(f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}-{\widetilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right)=\left\lVert{\widetilde{K}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{K}}}\right\rVert_{{\widetilde{H}}}.

∎

Corollary 23.6
.

For
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
{\boldsymbol{x}}\neq{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
for
0\leq i<n
, the posterior variance in (
36
) satisfies

\sup_{{\widetilde{f}}\in{\widetilde{H}}:\lVert{\widetilde{f}}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}}\leq 1}\left\lvert{\widetilde{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-f({\boldsymbol{x}})\nmid{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}}\right\rvert^{2}={\widetilde{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\mathrm{Var}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|\boldsymbol{f}]+\xi.

24
Fast GPs

In this section we will briefly review fast GPs using SI and DSI kernels along with special point sets which enable reduced computational costs and storage requirements. These ideas were originally proposed in
[
99
,
100
]
and more recently used in the context of fast Bayesian cubature with fast kernel hyperparameter optimization
[
70
,
71
,
72
]
, see
Section

3
for more background. We will defer our discussions on fast Bayesian cubature and fast kernel hyperparameter optimization to
Section

26
where fast multitask GPs are discussed. The standard GPs discussed earlier in this chapter and the fast GPs discussed here are a special case of their multitask formulations

Suppose we have
n=2^{m}
points with
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, and we are using one of the two following point set
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{2^{m}-1}
and kernel
K
pairings:

1.

A shifted rank-1 lattice in radical inverse order (
Section

11.1
) with an SI kernel (
Definition

22.1
).

2.

A base
2
digitally-shifted digital net in radical inverse order (
Section

11.2
) with a DSI kernel (
Definition

22.2
). Higher-order digital nets and/or linear matrix scrambling are permitted, but nested uniform scrambling is not allowed.

Recall the comparison between standard GPs and fast GPs given in
Table

6
.

Under such pairings,
Theorem

17.4
implies that the Gram matrix has an eigendecomposition

{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}={\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}},\qquad{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}={\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}{\boldsymbol{1}}=\sqrt{n}{\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{:,0}

where
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{:,0}
is the zeroth column of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
and
{\mathsf{V}}
is a fast transform matrix satisfying
17.1
. Then
Corollary

17.5
implies that for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we may write

{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}\odot{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}),\qquad{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}./{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}),\qquad\log\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert={\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}\log\lvert{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\rvert,\qquad\mathrm{trace}({\mathsf{K}})={\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}

where
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{y}}}=\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
, the elementwise (Hadamard) product is
\odot
, elementwise division is denoted by
./
, and
{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\intercal}\log\lvert{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\rvert=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\log\lvert{\widetilde{\lambda}}_{i}\rvert.
This implies fast GPs require only
{\mathcal{O}}(n)
storage and
{\mathcal{O}}(n\log n+dn)
computations to fit, including hyperparameter optimization via either NMLL (
Section

21.3
) or GCV (
Section

21.4
).

25
Multitask GPs

Often times one has access to simulations which permit evaluation at varying levels of fidelity. For example, a numerical PDE solver may be able to compute solutions with increasing mesh resolution and increasing cost. We will assume the dimension
d
is the same on each level, as is often the case for solvers of parameterized PDEs where
d
controls the number of terms kept in the Karhunen–Loève expansion. In such cases, we would like a GP surrogate to exploit both inter-task and intra-task covariance, where the tasks may be evaluations at different fidelity levels or more generally correlated objectives. The multitask GPs we describe here were proposed in
[
78
]
, see
Section

2.2
for additional background.

Suppose we have
L
correlated functions (tasks), each acting on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. We will denote this set of functions by

f:\{1,\dots,L\}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}},

where
f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})
evaluates task
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
at parameters
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Rather than fitting independent GPs to
f(1,\cdot),\dots,f(L,\cdot)
, we would like to fit a single GP which additionally exploits covariance information between tasks.

Like standard GPs, multitask GPs are defined with respect to an SPD kernel
K:(\{1,\dots,L\}\times{\mathcal{X}})\times(\{1,\dots,L\}\times{\mathcal{X}})\to{\mathbb{R}}
, see
Definition

16.1
, and a prior mean
M:\{1,\dots,L\}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Then our prior is

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{GP}(M,K),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})]

\displaystyle=M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),f(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})).

Suppose at level
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
we observe

{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{f}_{\ell}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\ell},\qquad\boldsymbol{f}_{\ell}=(f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i}))_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1},\qquad({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i})_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}\subset{\mathcal{X}},\qquad{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\ell}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\xi_{\ell}{\mathsf{I}}).

Here
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}
are noisy function evaluations at collocations points
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i})_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}
with IID zero-mean Gaussian noise
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\ell}
having common noise variance (nugget)
\xi_{\ell}>0
. We also assume
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{L}
are independent.

We will denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{K}}_{\ell}(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=(K((\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}),(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i})))_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\ell}},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{M}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=(M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i}))_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\ell}},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{K}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=(K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell^{\prime}i^{\prime}})))_{i,i^{\prime}=0}^{n_{\ell},n_{\ell^{\prime}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\ell}\times n_{\ell^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:={\mathsf{K}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}+\xi_{\ell}1_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}{\mathsf{I}}_{n_{\ell}}

where
{\mathsf{I}}_{n_{\ell}}
is the
n_{\ell}\times n_{\ell}
identity matrix and
1_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}
is
1
if
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
and
0
otherwise. Let
N=n_{1}+\dots+n_{L}
be the total number of observations. We will collect these level dependent vectors and matrices into block vectors and block matrices

\boldsymbol{f}:=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{f}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\qquad{\boldsymbol{y}}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\qquad{\boldsymbol{M}}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{M}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{M}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

{\boldsymbol{K}}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}):=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{K}}_{1}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})\\
\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{K}}_{L}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\qquad{\mathsf{K}}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{K}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{K}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{K}}_{L1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{K}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times N},

{\mathsf{\Xi}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}{\mathsf{I}}_{n_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\xi_{L}{\mathsf{I}}_{n_{L}}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times N},

{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}:={\mathsf{K}}+{\mathsf{\Xi}}\in{\boldsymbol{R}}^{N\times N}.

As with standard GPs, the posterior distribution of
f
after observing
{\boldsymbol{y}}
is also a GP. Using the above notations, we may write the posterior distribution as

\displaystyle f|{\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{GP}({\mathbb{E}}[f|{\boldsymbol{y}}],\mathrm{Cov}[f,f|{\boldsymbol{y}}]),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle=M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})+{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}\left[{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{M}}\right],

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),f(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle=K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}))-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\intercal}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}){\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{K}}(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}).

Without additional structure, multitask GPs cost
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{3}+dN^{2})
and require
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{2})
storage, including hyperparameter optimization. In
Section

26
we will show how these costs can be reduced using special SI/DSI kernels along with special point sets.

Let us introduce some additional notations to be used in the following subsections. We collect powers of inverse Gram matrices into blocks

{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}={\mathsf{A}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{A}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{A}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{A}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{A}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix},\qquad{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}={\mathsf{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{B}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{B}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}.

We will also use the “level summing” matrix

{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times L}

where
{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}
is the length
n_{\ell}
vector of
1
. For example, if
L=2
with
n_{1}=2
and
n_{2}=3
, then

{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&0\\
0&1\\
0&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{5\times 2}.

First,
Section

25.1
will discuss multitask GP hyperparameter optimization, then
Section

25.4
will discuss Bayesian cubature for multitask GPs, and finally
Section

25.5
will introduce product structure for multitask GP kernels.

25.1
Hyperparameter Optimization (HPOPT)

Here we will discuss HPOPT for multitask GPs which closely follows
Section

21.2
. As was the case there, we note that the kernel typically relies on hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, e.g.,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\gamma,{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathsf{G}},{\boldsymbol{t}}\}
where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
are smoothness parameters,
\gamma
is a global scaling parameter,
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
are lengthscales, and
{\mathsf{G}},{\boldsymbol{t}}
control inter-task correlations as we will discuss in
Section

25.5
. As was the case for standard GPs, we will discuss HPOPT via the NMLL (
Section

25.2
) or GCV (
Section

25.3
) loss.

25.2
HPOPT: Negative Marginal Log-Likelihood

Theorem 25.1
.

If the prior mean are constants
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{L})^{\intercal}
at each level, i.e.,
M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the NMLL is, up to an additive constant independent of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, proportional to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{NMLL}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}})

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}^{\intercal}-\tau_{1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{y}}_{L}^{\intercal}-\tau_{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{A}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{A}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{A}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{A}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}-\tau_{1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{L}-\tau_{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}

(45)

\displaystyle+\log\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert.

Moreover, the optimal prior mean constants
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
which minimizes the NMLL satisfy

\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}={\mathsf{T}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(46)

Proof.

Using our updated notations the NMLL is still proportional to (
37
), so

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{NMLL}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}-\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}){\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\tau_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})+\log\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert.

Setting the derivative with respect to
\tau_{\ell}
equal to
0
gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{NMLL}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}})/(\partial\tau_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial\left[({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}-\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}){\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}-\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell})+2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}-\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}){\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}-\tau_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})\right]/(\partial\tau_{\ell})

\displaystyle=2\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}-2{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}-2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\tau_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}),

so the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\neq\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell}+\frac{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle\therefore\;0

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}},

\displaystyle\therefore\;\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}.

Expanding over all
\ell
gives the desired linear system.
∎

25.3
HPOPT: Generalized Cross Validation

Theorem 25.2
.

If the prior mean are constants
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{L})^{\intercal}
at each level, i.e.,
M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the GCV loss is proportional to

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GCV}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=\frac{\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}^{\intercal}-\tau_{1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{y}}_{L}^{\intercal}-\tau_{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}}{\mathrm{trace}^{2}({\mathsf{A}})}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{B}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{B}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}-\tau_{1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{L}-\tau_{L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}.

(47)

Moreover, the optimal prior mean constants
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
which minimizes the GCV loss satisfies

\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
{\accentset{\star}{\tau}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}={\mathsf{T}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(48)

Proof.

Using our updated notations, the GCV loss is still proportional to (
38
),

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GCV}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\mathrm{trace}^{-2}({\mathsf{A}})\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}-\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}){\mathsf{B}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\tau_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}).

Setting the derivative with respect to
\tau_{\ell}
equal to
0
gives

\displaystyle 0=2\tau_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}-2{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell\ell}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}-2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\tau_{\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}).

The remaining steps directly follow the proof of
Theorem

25.1
with
{\mathsf{A}}
replaced by
{\mathsf{B}}
, so the result follows.
∎

25.4
Bayesian Cubature

We are often interested in a linear combination of integrals on each level. With weights
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L}
, we denote

\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\omega_{\ell}\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}.

The posterior distribution of
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
is the Gaussian

\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}],{\mathbb{V}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}])

with respective mean and variance

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}

where

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

\displaystyle={\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}+{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{M}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\approx}{{\mathsf{E}}}-{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L},

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L},

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}{\boldsymbol{K}}(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{\ell=1}^{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times L},

\displaystyle\accentset{\approx}{{\mathsf{E}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}))\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L}.

Theorem 25.3
.

Suppose we are given
{\boldsymbol{\chi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L}
, e.g.,
{\boldsymbol{\chi}}=(0,\dots,0,1)^{\intercal}
or
{\boldsymbol{\chi}}={\boldsymbol{1}}
. Let us denote the mean squared error (MSE)

\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})={\mathbb{E}}[(\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}])^{2}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]={\mathbb{V}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]+\mathrm{BIAS}^{2}(\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})

where

\mathrm{BIAS}(\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}})={\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}].

Then

{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}:=\argmin\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\chi}})\left({\mathsf{\Sigma}}+{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}.

(49)

Moreover,

\mathrm{MSE}({\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}})=({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\chi}})^{2}(1-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}({\mathsf{\Sigma}}+{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}).

Proof.

The bias variance decomposition is standard:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[(\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}])^{2}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]-2{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]+{\mathbb{E}}^{2}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{V}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]+{\mathbb{E}}^{2}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]-2{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]+{\mathbb{E}}^{2}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{V}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]+\left({\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nu_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}|{\boldsymbol{y}}]\right)^{2}.

To prove the minimizer, write

\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})={\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}+({\boldsymbol{\omega}}-{\boldsymbol{\chi}})^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}-{\boldsymbol{\chi}})

so that

\frac{\partial\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}=2{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}+2{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-2{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\chi}}=0

when

({\mathsf{\Sigma}}+{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}){\boldsymbol{\omega}}={\boldsymbol{\rho}}({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\chi}}).

Writing

\displaystyle\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})={\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{\intercal}({\mathsf{\Sigma}}+{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}){\boldsymbol{\omega}}+{\boldsymbol{\chi}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\chi}}-2{\boldsymbol{\chi}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}

and plugging in the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}
from (
49
) gives the expression for the optimal MSE.
∎

Notice that regardless of
{\boldsymbol{\chi}}
, the minimizer of
\mathrm{MSE}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})
is always proportional to
({\mathsf{\Sigma}}+{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\intercal})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
.

25.5
Product Structure

Almost all literature and implementations of multitask GPs assume
K
has a product structure

K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}))=R(\ell,\ell^{\prime})Q({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\;\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,L\},\quad\forall\;{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d}

(50)

for some kernels
R:\{1,\dots,L\}\times\{1,\dots,L\}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
Q:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Here
Q
is taken to be a standard kernel such as those in
Section

22
, e.g., a squared exponential or (digitally-)shift-invariant one. The kernel
R
is typically parameterized directly by a low rank matrix
{\mathsf{G}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times s}
with
s\in\{0,\dots,L\}
and a diagonal vector
{\boldsymbol{t}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{L}
so that the Gram matrix is

{\mathsf{R}}=(R(\ell,\ell^{\prime}))_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}={\mathsf{G}}{\mathsf{G}}^{\intercal}+\mathrm{diag}({\boldsymbol{t}}).

26
Fast Multitask GPs

This section extends the fast GPs formulation in
Section

24
to the multitask setting. Of course, the single task setting is a special case of this multitask formulation. The resulting structures and algorithms we derive here are not available elsewhere in the literature.

Let us assume we are using a product kernel as in (
50
) and have sample sizes
n_{\ell}=2^{m_{\ell}}
for
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, so the total number of points is
N=n_{1}+\dots n_{L}
. We will enable fast multitask GPs by either

1.

choosing
Q
to be a shift-invariant (SI) kernel (
Definition

22.1
) and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell i})_{i=0}^{2^{m_{\ell}}-1}
to be shifted rank-1 lattices in radical inverse order with possibly different shifts on each level, or

2.

choosing
Q
to be a digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernel (
Definition

22.2
) and
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i})_{i=0}^{2^{m_{\ell}}-1}
to be digitally-shifted base
2
digital nets in radical inverse order with possibly different digital shifts on each level. As the generating matrices must be the same on each level, a single linear matrix scramble (LMS) may be applied to all generating matrices, but different LMS on each level is
not
permitted. Nested uniform scrambling (NUS) is also prohibited.

Of course, this requires the domain to be
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1)^{d}
.

After potentially reordering levels, let us assume
n_{1}\geq\cdots\geq n_{L}
without loss of generality. Then, under either of the two special pairings above, we may use
Lemma

17.2
and
Theorem

17.4
to say that for any
1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq L
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{V}}_{\ell}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}=\sqrt{2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell}}({\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{:,0}.

Here
{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell}:={\mathsf{V}}_{m_{\ell}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2^{m_{\ell}}\times 2^{m_{\ell}}}
are fast transform matrices satisfying
17.1
,
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
is a
2^{m_{\ell}-m_{\ell^{\prime}}}\times 1
block matrix with
2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}}\times 2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}}
diagonal blocks, and
({\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{:,0}
is the zeroth column of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we may write

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{1}}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{1L}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{L}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{V}}_{L}\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{1L}}\;\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{1}}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{L}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{LL}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{L}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&{\mathsf{V}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}&\cdots&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{1L}}&\cdots&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{L}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=:{\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}.

We will often denote the collection of the first
\ell
row blocks and first
\ell^{\prime}
column blocks by

{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:\ell,:\ell^{\prime}}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}&\cdots&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{1\ell^{\prime}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell 1}}&\cdots&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}

Both
{\mathsf{V}},\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
are fixed and known. Multiplication by
{\mathsf{V}},\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}
can be done quickly using fast transforms. As detailed in
Section

15.4.3
, the fast transforms are

1.

the FFTBR/IFFTBR algorithms in the case of SI kernels and shifted rank-1 lattices in radical inverse order, and

2.

the FWHT algorithm in the case of DSI kernels and base
2
digitally-shifted digital nets in radical inverse order.

It is therefore sufficient to work with the sparse matrix
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
. Multiplication by
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
is straightforward. In order to fit a multitask GP, we will also need to invert
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
and compute the determinant.

To motivate our fast inverse and determinant algorithm, let us consider an example with
L=3
tasks having
n_{1}=8
,
n_{2}=4
and
n_{3}=2
. Then
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
has the following sparsity pattern

{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{13}\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{22}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{23}\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{13}}&\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{23}}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{33}\end{pmatrix}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccccc|cccc|cc}.&&&&&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&.&&&&&&&&.&&&&.\\
&&.&&&&&&&&.&&.&\\
&&&.&&&&&&&&.&&.\\
&&&&.&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&&&&&.&&&&.&&&&.\\
&&&&&&.&&&&.&&.&\\
&&&&&&&.&&&&.&&.\\
\hline\cr.&&&&.&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&.&&&&.&&&&.&&&&.\\
&&.&&&&.&&&&.&&.&\\
&&&.&&&&.&&&&.&&.\\
\hline\cr.&&.&&.&&.&&.&&.&&.&\\
&.&&.&&.&&.&&.&&.&&.\end{array}\right].

(51)

Then

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{22}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}+{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{-1}\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}&-{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{-1}\\
-{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{-1}\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}&{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccccc|cccc}.&&&&.&&&&.&&&\\
&.&&&&.&&&&.&&\\
&&.&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&&&.&&&&.&&&&.\\
.&&&&.&&&&.&&&\\
&.&&&&.&&&&.&&\\
&&.&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&&&.&&&&.&&&&.\\
\hline\cr.&&&&.&&&&.&&&\\
&.&&&&.&&&&.&&\\
&&.&&&&.&&&&.&\\
&&&.&&&&.&&&&.\end{array}\right]

where

{\mathsf{S}}_{2}={\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{22}-\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{12}

is the Schur complement. We also have that

\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}\rvert=\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{11}\rvert\cdot\lvert{\mathsf{S}}_{2}\rvert.

Then in the next step we may say

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}={\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:3,:3}^{-1}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}\\
\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}}&{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{33}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}+{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}{\mathsf{S}}_{3}^{-1}\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:1}^{-1}&-{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}{\mathsf{S}}_{3}^{-1}\\
{\mathsf{S}}_{3}^{-1}\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}&{\mathsf{S}}_{3}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

where

{\mathsf{S}}_{3}={\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{33}-\overline{{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,3}

is another Schur complement and

\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert=\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:3,:3}\rvert=\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:2,:2}\rvert\cdot\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{3}\rvert.

Crucially,
{\mathsf{S}}_{\ell}
are diagonal and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}_{:\ell,:\ell}
only depends on
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}_{:\ell-1,:\ell-1}
,
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:\ell-1,\ell}
, and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{\ell\ell}
.

Algorithm

2
gives a general routine for computing
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
and
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert
. Then,
Theorem

26.1
analyzes the cost and storage requirements for computing
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
, multiplying by
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
, inverting
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
, and finding the determinant of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
.

Algorithm 2

Compute the Inverse
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
and Determinant
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert

{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
diagonal block matrix with
m_{1}\geq\dots\geq m_{L}
.

\triangleright
A
2^{m_{1}}\times 2^{m_{1}}
diagonal matrix.

\triangleright
A
2^{m_{1}}\times 2^{m_{1}}
diagonal matrix. Costs
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{m_{1}})
.

\triangleright

.

\ell\leftarrow 2

\ell\leq L

do

\triangleright
A
2^{m_{\ell}}\times 2^{m_{\ell}}
diagonal matrix.

\triangleright
A
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}\times 1

2^{m_{\ell}}\times 2^{m_{\ell}}

\triangleright
A
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}\times 1

.

.

. Equivalent to

diagonal matrix. Costs

.

.

\triangleright
A
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}\times\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}
block matrix with
2^{m_{\ell}}\times 2^{m_{\ell}}
diagonal blocks. Costs
{\mathcal{O}}([\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}][\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}}])
.

{\mathsf{A}}\leftarrow\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{M}}&-{\mathsf{H}}\\
-\overline{{\mathsf{H}}}^{\intercal}&{\mathsf{G}}\end{pmatrix}

\triangleright
A
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}\times\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}2^{m_{\ell^{\prime}}-m_{\ell}}
block matrix with
2^{m_{\ell}}\times 2^{m_{\ell}}
diagonal blocks. Equivalent to
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}_{:\ell,:\ell}^{-1}
.

\ell\leftarrow\ell+1

\triangleright
A
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}2^{m_{\ell}-m_{L}}\times\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}2^{m_{\ell}-m_{L}}
block matrix with
2^{m_{L}}\times 2^{m_{L}}
diagonal blocks.

\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert\leftarrow\varrho

\triangleright
A scalar.

return

{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1},\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert

Theorem 26.1
.

Assume
n_{\ell}=2^{m_{\ell}}
with
m_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and, without loss of generality, assume
n_{1}\geq\cdots\geq n_{L}
and
N=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{L}
. Evaluating
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
requires

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(L-\ell+1)(n_{\ell}\log(n_{\ell})+dn_{\ell})\right)\text{ computations and }

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(L-\ell+1)n_{\ell}\right)\text{ storage}.

Computing
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}n_{\ell}\log(n_{\ell})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(L-\ell+1)n_{\ell}\right)\text{ computations}.

Then, evaluating
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert=\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert
and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
using
Algorithm

2
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\left(\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}n_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)^{2}/n_{\ell}\right)\text{ computations and }{\mathcal{O}}(N^{2}/n_{L})\text{ storage}.

Having stored
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
, evaluating
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}n_{\ell}\log(n_{\ell})+N^{2}/n_{L}\right)\text{ computations}.

Corollary 26.2
.

Assume
n:=n_{\ell}=2^{m}
with
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
for all
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
so
N=Ln
. Evaluating
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N\times N}
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{2}(n\log n+dn)\right)\text{ computations and }{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{2}n\right)\text{ storage}.

Computing
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(Ln\log n+L^{2}n\right)\text{ computations}.

Then, evaluating
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert=\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\rvert
and
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
using
Algorithm

2
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{3}n\right)\text{ computations and }{\mathcal{O}}(L^{2}n)\text{ storage}.

Having stored
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}
, evaluating
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}={\mathsf{V}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}{\boldsymbol{y}}
for any
{\boldsymbol{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
requires

{\mathcal{O}}\left(Ln\log n+L^{2}n\right)\text{ computations}.

Let us introduce some additional notations useful in the next subsections. Similar to what was done for standard multitask GPs, we will collect powers of
{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
into blocks

{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-1}={\mathsf{\Gamma}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{00}&\cdots&{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix},\qquad{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}^{-2}={\mathsf{\Upsilon}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{00}&\cdots&{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix},

so that

{\mathsf{A}}={\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}={\mathsf{V}}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}},\qquad{\mathsf{B}}={\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-2}={\mathsf{V}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}}.

Moreover, let us collect

{\mathsf{\Pi}}=\left(\sqrt{n_{\ell}n_{\ell^{\prime}}}({\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{00}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L},\qquad{\mathsf{P}}=\left(\sqrt{n_{\ell}n_{\ell^{\prime}}}({\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{00}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L}

where
({\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{00}
and
({\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{00}
are the upper left elements of
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
and
{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
respectively.

Section

26.1
will discuss hyperparameter optimization (HPOPT) for fast multitask GPs and then
Section

26.4
will discuss fast Bayesian cubature. Both HPOPT and Bayesian cubature for fast multitask GPs do not generally increase the cost or storage requirements beyond that of solving linear system in
{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
and computing the determinant
\lvert{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}\rvert
, both of which were analyzed in
Theorem

26.1
.

26.1
Hyperparameter Optimization (HPOPT)

This section closely follows
Section

25.1
which discussed HPOPT for standard multitask GPs via either the NMLL (
Section

26.2
) or GCV (
Section

26.3
) losses.

26.2
HPOPT: Negative Marginal Log-Likelihood (NMLL)

Theorem 26.3
.

When the prior mean are constants
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{L})^{\intercal}
at each level, i.e.,
M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1]^{d}
, then the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
which minimizes the NMLL (
45
) satisfies

{\mathsf{\Pi}}{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}={\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{A}}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

Proof.

Recall the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
in (
46
) and that

{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}={\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell^{\prime}}}=\sqrt{n_{\ell}}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell^{\prime}}}=\sqrt{n_{\ell}}({\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{0,:}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell^{\prime}}},

{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}={\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell^{\prime}}=\sqrt{n_{\ell}n_{\ell^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\ell}=\sqrt{n_{\ell}n_{\ell^{\prime}}}=\sqrt{n_{\ell}n_{\ell^{\prime}}}({\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{00}

where
{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\ell}=(1,0,0,\dots)^{\intercal}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\ell}}
and
({\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}})_{0,:}
is the zeroth row of
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
.
∎

26.3
HPOPT: Generalized Cross Validation (GCV)

Theorem 26.4
.

When the prior mean are constants
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{L})^{\intercal}
at each level, i.e.,
M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\tau_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1]^{d}
, then the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
which minimizes the GCV loss (
47
) satisfies

{\mathsf{P}}{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}={\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{A}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

Proof.

Recalling the optimal
{\accentset{\star}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
in (
48
), the proof is analogous to that of
Theorem

26.3
but with
{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
replaced by
{\mathsf{B}},{\mathsf{\Upsilon}}
respectively.
∎

26.4
Bayesian Cubature

Recall that for the product-form SI kernels in
Definition

22.1
or product-form DSI kernels in
Definition

22.2
that

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}Q({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}Q({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\gamma

for any
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1)^{d}
where
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
is the global scaling parameter.

Theorem 26.5
.

Following the notation in
Section

25.4
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=\gamma{\mathsf{R}}-\gamma^{2}{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{\Pi}}{\mathsf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L}.

Proof.

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{R}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times L}

\displaystyle\accentset{\approx}{{\mathsf{E}}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}{\mathsf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times L}

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}^{\intercal}{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}^{-1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}}=

\displaystyle q^{2}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{R}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&{\mathsf{V}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{11}&\cdots&{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{L1}&\ddots&{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\overline{{\mathsf{V}}_{L}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{R}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{1}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{L}^{\intercal}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{R}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{L}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}n_{1}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{11}&\cdots&\sqrt{n_{1}n_{L}}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{1L}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sqrt{n_{L}n_{1}}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{L1}&\ddots&n_{L}{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{LL}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{11}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{1L}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathsf{R}}_{L1}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{l}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{LL}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{L}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{\Pi}}{\mathsf{R}}

where again
{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\ell}=(1,0,0,\dots)^{\intercal}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\ell}}
.
∎

27
Derivative-Informed GPs

Here we discuss GPs with derivative information
[
51
, Chapter 9.4]
, which may be seen as a special case of optimal recovery in Hilbert spaces as discussed in
[
204
, Chapter 16]
.
Section

2.3
provides additional background on GPs which incorporate derivatives. Our development of derivative informed fast GPs using SI/DSI kernels is novel in the literature.

27.1
Background on Derivatives of GPs

Definition 27.1
.

A process
f
is mean squared differentiable if the following limit exists:

\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{f(x+h_{1})-f(x)}{h_{1}}-\frac{f(x+h_{2})-f(x)}{h_{2}}\right)^{2}\right].

When the limits are replaced by right or left limits the process is said to be right or left mean squared differentiable respectively.

Lemma 27.2
.

If a SPSD kernel
K:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies
K(x,x)=K(0,0)
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, then a Gaussian process
f\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,K)
is mean squared differentiable if and only if both the following limit exists:

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(x+h,x)-K(x,x)}{h^{2}}\qquad\text{and}

\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{K(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-K(x+h_{1},x)-K(x+h_{2},x)+K(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}.

Right and left mean squared differentiability follow from replacing limits by right and left limits respectively.

Proof.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle\left[\left(\frac{f(x+h_{1})-f(x)}{h_{1}}-\frac{f(x+h_{2})-f(x)}{h_{2}}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{f(x+h_{1})-f(x)}{h_{1}}\right)^{2}\right]+{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{f(x+h_{2})-f(x)}{h_{2}}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle-2{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{f(x+h_{1})-f(x)}{h_{1}}\cdot\frac{f(x+h_{2})-f(x)}{h_{2}}\right].

Now,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left[f^{2}(x+h)-2f(x+h)f(x)+f^{2}(x)\right]}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(x+h,x+h)-2K(x+h,x)+K(x,x)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(x,x)-K(x+h,x)}{h^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{f(x+h_{1})-f(x)}{h_{1}}\cdot\frac{f(x+h_{2})-f(x)}{h_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{f(x+h_{1})f(x+h_{2})-f(x+h_{1})f(x)-f(x+h_{2})f(x)+f^{2}(x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{h_{1},h_{2}\to 0}\frac{K(x+h_{1},x+h_{2})-K(x+h_{1},x)-K(x+h_{2},x)+K(x,x)}{h_{1}h_{2}}

∎

If a process
f
is mean squared differentiable, we will denote its derivative process as
f^{(1)}
. If
f^{(1)}
is mean squared differentiable, then we will denote its derivative by
f^{(2)}
, and so on. If it exists, we will say
f^{(\beta)}
is the
\beta^{th}
derivative of the process
f
. For multidimensional domains
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, as we assume going forward, we will denote
f^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}
, with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
, to be the process taking
\beta_{j}
derivatives in dimension
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
.

When a function is mean squared differentiable, then, as differentiation is a linear operator, we may apply the derivatives directly to the kernel. The squared exponential kernel
Definition

22.3
is infinitely mean squared differentiable. Discussions on the differentiability of other kernels are readily available, see for example
[
52
,
205
]
. When GP derivatives exist, the required kernel derivatives may often be readily obtained using automatic differentiation.

27.2
Connection to Multitask GPs

Suppose we have
L
compatible derivative multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{L}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
, and we observe at level
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
derivative observations

{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\ell}=f^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell})}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\ell}.

As with the multitask GPs discussed in
Section

25
, we will assume IID zero-mean Gaussian noise
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\ell}
with common noise variance (nugget)
\xi_{\ell}>0
. For computational purposes, we will also assume
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{L}
are independent as was the case for multitask GPs.

To construct a GP over
f
which incorporates derivative observations, we must define a prior mean
M:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a prior covariance kernel
K:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
which are
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell}
times mean squared differentiable for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
. For non-zero prior means, differentiability still holds as long as the prior mean is sufficiently differentiable. GPs with derivatives can be seen as a special case of the multitask GPs in
Section

25
with

\displaystyle M(\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle:=M^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell})}({\boldsymbol{x}}),

\displaystyle K((\ell,{\boldsymbol{x}}),(\ell^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}))

\displaystyle:=K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})

for all
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,L\}
.

27.3
Fast Derivative-Informed GPs

The following corollary concerns derivatives of GPs with our product-form shift-invariant (SI) and digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernels which have
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1]^{d}
. As these kernels are of product-form, their per-dimension differentiability holds for their multidimensional forms.

Corollary 27.3
.

Suppose the GP prior mean
M:[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is sufficiently mean squared differentiable, e.g., the constant means we considered earlier.

1.

For the product-form SI kernel
K
in
Definition

22.1
built from one-dimensional kernels
{\widetilde{K}}_{\alpha_{j}}
with smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>1/2}^{d}
, the GP
f\sim\mathrm{GP}(M,K)
is mean squared differentiable
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
times, i.e.,
f^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}
exists, so long as
2{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}>1
elementwise. The derivative forms are described in
Definition

22.2
.

2.

For the product-form DSI kernel
K
in
Definition

22.2
built from one-dimensional kernels
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha_{j},0,0}
with smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{2,3,4\}^{d}
, the GP
f\sim\mathrm{GP}(M,K)
is not mean squared differentiable. However, if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\{3,4\}^{d}
, then the posterior mean is right differentiable on all dyadic rationals
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
with derivative given in
Corollary

20.34
.

3.

For the product-form DSI kernel
K
in
Definition

22.2
built from one-dimensional kernels
{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha_{j}}
with smoothness parameters
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{d}
, the GP
f\sim\mathrm{GP}(M,K)
is mean squared differentiable
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
times, i.e.,
f^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}
exists, so long as
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}>2
elementwise. All derivatives, when they exist, are equal to
0
, see
Theorem

20.35
.

Proof.

All claims use
Lemma

27.2
. The claim regarding SI kernels follows from applying
Theorem

19.6
. The claim regarding the first DSI kernel built from
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha_{j},0,0}
follows from
Theorem

20.36
. The claim regarding the second DSI kernel built from
{\dddot{K}}_{\alpha}
follows from
Theorem

20.37
.
∎

All the technology developed in
Section

26
for fast multitask GPs immediately applies to fast derivative-informed GPs. The only requirement is that the derivative kernels
K^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\ell^{\prime}})}
are still shift-invariant (SI) if we are using lattices, or digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) if we are using digital nets.
Definition

22.1
shows this is in fact the case for SI kernels. We have yet to find practically useful DSI kernels which are also mean squared differentiable.

28
Numerical Experiments

The numerical experiments use our
FastGPs
package (
https://alegresor.github.io/fastgps/
) version 1.0 or later, which relies on both the
QMCPy
and
PyTorch
packages.
FastGPs
is readily installed using the command
pip install -U fastgps
, after which we may import our required packages:

⬇

import

fastgps

as

fgps

import

qmcpy

as

qp

import

torch

While we do not provide code snippets here, extensive documentation and examples are available at the project URL above.
Section

29
contains additional experiments using the fast Bayesian cubature support in
FastGPs
to enable multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo without replications.

Our experiments use the
\alpha=2
SI product kernel in
Definition

22.1
and
\alpha=4
DSI product kernel in
Definition

22.2
with higher-order univariate kernels
{\ddot{K}}_{\alpha,0,0}
. LD point sets are generated using
QMCPy
’s default generating vector for lattices and default generating matrices for digital nets. We found our results to be robust to these choices.
All optimizations of the MLL were run to convergence using the resilient back propagation (Rprop) algorithm
[
206
]
with learning rate
0.1
. We found optimization of the SI and DSI kernels converged in orders of magnitude fewer iterations than for squared exponential (SE) kernels.
Accuracy will be measured by the
L_{2}
relative error

\sqrt{\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left\lvert{\accentset{\star}{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})-{\mathbb{E}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}/\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left\lvert{\accentset{\star}{f}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}}

(52)

between the true benchmark functions
{\accentset{\star}{f}}
and the posterior means
{\mathbb{E}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]
. This is approximated numerically using the
\ell_{2}
norm and LD Halton points. All computations were performed on the CPUs of a 2023 MacBook Pro with an M3 Max processor.

We are interested in comparing zero-mean GPs with SE, SI, and DSI kernels both with and without gradient information. We note that our benchmarks are smooth, two-sided differentiable functions which do not necessarily align with our fast GP modeling assumptions. Rather than benchmarking only on functions for which fast GPs are theoretically justified, we are instead interested in seeing how our methods apply to more realistic functions where our kernel assumptions may not hold.

28.1
Low-dimensional functions without derivatives

We begin in the low-dimensional setting without derivative information and compare across SE GPs with lattice points, fast SI GPs with lattice points, and fast DSI GPs with digital nets.
Figure

18
considers a set of visually diverse
d=1
curves.
Figure

5
considers the
d=2
-dimensional Ackley function
[
109
]
. Notice the periodicity of SI predictions and the discontinuity of DSI predictions as reflected in the kernels.

Figure 18
:
Comparison of Gaussian process (GP) methods on visually diverse example functions in dimension
d=1
. Black lines are the true functions, colored lines are the posterior means, and shaded regions show 99% confidence intervals (CIs). The figure lists the
L_{2}
relative errors, the average CI width, the proportion of inputs for which the CI captures the true function, and the optimized MLL.

For the
d=1
examples, similar accuracies and MLLs were achieved by all three methods. SE and SI GPs yield similar
99\%
confidence intervals (CIs)

{\mathbb{E}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]\pm 2.58\sqrt{{\mathbb{V}}[f({\boldsymbol{x}})|{\boldsymbol{y}}]}

(53)

as evidence by their similar (average) CI widths and CI capture proportions. DSI GPs are often less confident in their predictions, but the CI capture proportions always match the desired 99% confidence. SE GPs tend to be overconfident as evidenced by the low capture fractions. SI GPs tend to yield moderately sized CIs with moderate coverage while DSI GPs give large CIs with high coverage.

For the
d=2
Ackley function fit to
n=4096
points, our fast SI and DSI GP methods achieved slightly better accuracy than the classic SE GP method and were around
2000
and
1000
times faster respectively. However, the optimized MLL was over
6000
for SE GP and only around
-0.004
for SI and DSI GPs.

shows accuracy, fitting times, and marginal log-likelihoods (MLLs) for GPs using
n=1024
points both with and without gradient information on a number of synthetic benchmarks from the Virtual Library of Simulation Experiments
[

]
. For the SI kernel, the periodizing baker transform
\phi(x)=1-2\lvert x-1/2\rvert
was used to improve estimates for the gradient-informed Branin and Hartmann functions.
