Here we found results to be highly dependent on the benchmark. On the simpler
d=2
Branin, Camel, and Styblinski–Tang (StyTang) benchmarks, SE was orders of magnitude more accurate than SI and DSI GPs both with and without gradient information. However, on the highly oscillatory
d=1
Ackley function, SI is able to achieve comparable accuracy, and SI was able to efficiently utilize gradient information while SE cannot.
Moreover, on the
d=6
Hartmann example, all three methods attain similar accuracy without gradient information. SI was not able to utilize gradient information on either the Camel or StyTang benchmarks. This lack of efficiency may be attributed to the benchmarks not satisfying the assumptions of fast GPs as evidenced by the lower MLLs for SI/DSI GPs compared to SE GPs.

For GPs on
n=1024
points without gradients, the SI GP was consistently around
100
times faster than the SE GP. Including gradients at each of the
n=1024
points makes SI over
350
times faster than SE on
d=2
examples and almost
1700
times as fast on the
d=6
Hartmann function. DSI typically takes around twice as long as SI, partially due to the requirement to compute the DSI sum term
S
in (
30
). The
\alpha=2
and
\alpha=3
DSI kernels are faster to evaluate and did not significantly degrade accuracy in our testing.

28.3
Accuracy vs timing vs dimension for StyTang

Finally, in
Figure

19
we track effects of dimension
d\in\{1,2,4,8,16\}
and sample size
n
on the accuracy and time for the Styblinski–Tang benchmark
[
207
]
. We found that as
d
increases the SI and DSI GPs become significantly more accurate than the SE GPs. For SI kernels and small
n
, the benefit of gradient information appears to increase with
d
; this benefit is diminished at a horizon for
n
that also appears to increase with
d
. In other words, we found gradient information for fast GPs to be most valuable for large
d
and small
n
.

Figure 19
:
Gaussian processes (GPs) of varying dimension
d
on the Styblinski–Tang benchmark function. Here we compare the error, number of samples
n
, time per optimization step, and inclusion of gradient information
\nabla f
. GP fitting was run for
250
optimization steps.

\Chapter

Applications in UQ and SciML

This chapter will highlight a number of application projects in:

1.

Building fast Gaussian processes for multilevel Monte Carlo without replications (
Section

29
).

2.

Using Gaussian process to predict probability of system failure (
Section

30
).

3.

Solving the Darcy flow equation using multilevel Gaussian processes (
Section

31
).

4.

Modeling radiative heat transfer using neural operators (
Section

32
).

5.

Solving nonlinear PDEs with random coefficients to machine precision accuracy using scientific machine learning (
Section

33
).

Each section corresponds to a paper written with collaborators across academia, national labs, and industry. We will focus on the technical contributions of each project. The full papers contain additional problem context and connections to the broader literature.

29
Fast Bayesian Multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo

This section follows
[
17
]
, a paper I worked on during my 2025 DOE SCGSR (US Department of Energy Office of Science Graduate Student Research) Program fellowship in collaboration with Pieterjan Robbe, Fred J. Hickernell, Gianluca Geraci, and Michael S. Eldred.

Existing multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo (MLQMC) methods use multiple independent randomizations of a low-discrepancy (LD) sequence in order to compute error estimates on each level. Using multiple randomizations is inherently less efficient than sampling more points from a single LD sequence. We propose to cast the MLQMC problem into a Bayesian cubature framework which requires only a single LD sequence and provides error estimates in terms of the posterior variance. Moreover, using
Section

24
we may perform Gaussian process (GP) regression at only
{\mathcal{O}}(n\log n)
cost in the number of points
n
, including hyperparameter optimization, by pairing certain LD sequences to matching kernel forms. As existing MLQMC procedures iteratively double the sample size on the level of maximum utility, we develop a novel utility function for fast Bayesian MLQMC which trades off the cost of doubling and the forecasted posterior variance after doubling, assuming fixed GP hyperparameters. We also showcase a new digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernel of adaptive smoothness for use with fast digital net GPs which takes a hyperparameter-weighted sum of existing higher-order smoothness DSI kernels. A series of numerical experiment validate the performance of our fast Bayesian approximations and error estimates for both single-level problems and multilevel problems with a fixed number of levels. We find the proposed fast Bayesian MLQMC methods indeed provide superior approximations compared to standard MLQMC methods with multiple randomizations, and the Bayesian error estimates using digital nets are accurate for the true errors.

Quasi-Monte Carlo was detailed in
Section

8
with an initial overview of multilevel Monte Carlo (MLMC)
[
157
,
156
]
and multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo (MLQMC)
[
34
]
methods available in
Section

14.5
. We will compare against the classic MLQMC method with replications which on each level employs the replicated QMC estimator described in
Section

14.2
.
Section

24
provided background on fast GPs with low-discrepancy points and matching (digitally-)shift-invariant kernels, their hyperparameter optimization, and their application to fast Bayesian cubature. A unified introduction is give in the full paper
[
17
]
.

The remainder of this section is organized as follows.
Section

29.1
details our methods. We describe the existing IID-MLMC method in
Section

29.1.1
, then we describe the existing MLQMC method with multiple randomizations (replications) in
Section

29.1.2
, and then we describe fast Bayesian cubature along with our proposed fast Bayesian MLQMC method in
Section

29.1.3
. Algorithms are provided for each of these three methods. Afterwards,
Section

29.2
gives numerical experiments comparing the three algorithms for both single-level problems and multilevel problems with a fixed number of levels. Here will only present results for the two QMC methods using digital nets. Results for lattices are available in the full text
[
17
]
.

29.1
Methods

Assume we have access to quantities of interest
f_{\ell}:[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
where
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
indexes the fidelity of the simulation, with larger
\ell
corresponding to higher-fidelity simulations. Sometimes the dimension
d
varies with the level so
f_{\ell}:[0,1]^{d_{\ell}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
; for example,
d_{\ell}
may be the number of monitoring times of an asset price for option pricing with
d_{\ell}
increasing in
\ell
. In such cases, we take
d=\max(d_{1},\dots,d_{L})
and subset dimensions accordingly. We will also assume the number of levels is fixed, although extensions to adaptive multilevel schemes where the highest-fidelity level is determined by the remaining bias should be straightforward
[
157
]
.

We are interested in approximating the expectation of the maximum-fidelity simulation
\nu:={\mathbb{E}}[f_{L}({\boldsymbol{X}})]
subject to
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
. For problems with non-uniform stochasticity, one may employ a change of variables to produce a compatible form, see
Section

12
. As is standard for MLMC methods, we will expand the above quantity of interest as a telescoping sum

\nu={\mathbb{E}}[f_{L}({\boldsymbol{X}})]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}[f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})-f_{\ell-1}({\boldsymbol{X}})]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}[Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mu_{\ell}

(54)

where
Y_{\ell}:=f_{\ell}-f_{\ell-1}
,
\mu_{\ell}:={\mathbb{E}}[Y_{\ell}]
, and we set
f_{0}=0
so
Y_{1}=f_{1}
.
We will denote by
C_{\ell}
the cost of evaluating
Y_{\ell}
and by
V_{\ell}:={\mathbb{V}}[Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})]
the variance of the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
difference, again subject to
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
.

29.1.1
Multilevel Monte Carlo with Independent Points

Classic Monte Carlo methods approximate the true mean of an expectation by the
n
-sample mean at IID (independent and identically distributed) sampling locations. Standard MLMC methods
[
156
]
use IID samples on each level,

{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1,0},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1,n_{1}-1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{L,0},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{L,n_{L}-1}\overset{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim}{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d},

to create the estimate

{\widehat{\nu}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}\qquad\mathrm{where}\qquad{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i}).

(55)

This gives
{\mathbb{V}}[{\widehat{\nu}}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}V_{\ell}/n_{\ell}
, which, for a given error tolerance on
{\mathbb{V}}[{\widehat{\nu}}]
, will optimally allocate
n_{\ell}\propto\sqrt{V_{\ell}/C_{\ell}}
. As
V_{\ell}
is not known, it is typically approximated by an unbiased estimator. For consistency with the QMC schemes we will present in
Section

29.1.2
and
Section

29.1.3
, we will use an iterative doubling version of this IID-MLMC scheme as detailed in
Algorithm

3
.

Algorithm 3

MC
: MLMC With Independent Points

N>0

\triangleright
the budget

C_{1},\dots,C_{L}>0

\triangleright
the cost of evaluating
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{L}
respectively

n_{1}^{\mathrm{next}},\dots,n_{L}^{\mathrm{next}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}n_{\ell}^{\mathrm{next}}C_{\ell}\leq N

\triangleright
the initial sample sizes

n_{\ell}\leftarrow 0
for
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}

\triangleright
the number of model evaluations on each level

{\mathcal{L}}\leftarrow\{1,\dots,L\}

\triangleright
the set of levels to update

while
true
do

Generate
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
for
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
n_{\ell}\leq i<n_{\ell}^{\mathrm{next}}
all independently

Evaluate
Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i})
for
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
n_{\ell}\leq i<n_{\ell}^{\mathrm{next}}

{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i})
for
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}

\triangleright
estimate of
\mu_{\ell}

\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n_{\ell}-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}(Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i})-{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell})^{2}
for
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}

\triangleright
estimate of
V_{\ell}

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{feasible}}\leftarrow\{\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}:\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}C_{\ell^{\prime}}n_{\ell^{\prime}}+C_{\ell}n_{\ell}\leq N\}

\triangleright
feasible set of levels

if

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{feasible}}=\emptyset

then
break

\triangleright
exit loop before going over budget

\accentset{\star}{\ell}\leftarrow\argmax_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{feasible}}}\frac{\sigma_{\ell}^{2}}{n_{\ell}C_{\ell}}

\triangleright
choose the level of maximum utility

{\mathcal{L}}\leftarrow\{\accentset{\star}{\ell}\}
and
n_{\accentset{\star}{\ell}}\leftarrow n_{\accentset{\star}{\ell}}^{\mathrm{next}}
and
n_{\accentset{\star}{\ell}}^{\mathrm{next}}\leftarrow 2n_{\accentset{\star}{\ell}}

\triangleright
double samples on the chosen level

{\widehat{\nu}}\leftarrow\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}

\triangleright
estimate of
\nu

\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widehat{\sigma}}_{\ell}^{2}/n_{\ell}

\triangleright
estimate of
{\mathbb{V}}\left[{\widehat{\nu}}\right]

return

{\widehat{\nu}},\sigma,\{n_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}

\triangleright
the estimate, its standard error, and samples

29.1.2
Multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo with Replications

For Quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) methods, we will consider shifted rank-1 lattices and digitally-shifted base
2
digital nets, both in the extensible radical inverse order. These low-discrepancy sequences were plotted alongside IID points in
Figure

1
. From the unrandomized lattice or digital sequence
({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
, we will write the (digitally) shifted point set
({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
with
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
and shift
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\in[0,1)^{d}
. For fixed
({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
, if
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
then
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
. For lattices
\oplus
corresponds to addition modulo
1
, while for digital nets
\oplus
performs the elementwise exclusive or (XOR) between binary expansions. See
Section

11.1
and
Section

11.2
for complete definitions and additional details on lattices and digital nets respectively.

The multilevel QMC (MLQMC) method from
[
34
]
uses
R
independent shifts on each level

{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,R},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{L,1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{L,r}\overset{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim}{\mathcal{U}}[0,1)^{d}

to construct
LR
replications of LD point sets

({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,r,i})_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1},\qquad{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,r,i}:={\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\ell,r},\qquad 1\leq\ell\leq L,\quad 1\leq r\leq R.

(56)

One then replaces
{\widehat{\nu}}
in (
55
) by an approximation derived from the
LR
independent sample means
{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell,r}
:

{\widehat{\nu}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}\qquad\mathrm{where}\qquad{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{r=1}^{R}{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell,r}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell,r}=\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,r,i}).

Let
{\widetilde{V}}_{\ell,n_{\ell}}={\mathbb{V}}[1/n_{\ell}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})]
be the variance of the LD sample mean with respect to the random shift
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
, which should go to
0
as
n_{\ell}\to\infty
. Then we have
{\mathbb{V}}\left[{\widehat{\nu}}\right]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widetilde{V}}_{\ell,n_{\ell}}/R
. Rather than increasing the number of randomizations
R
, it will be more efficient to increase the number of LD point
n_{\ell}
and exploit the space filling properties of extensible LD sequences. We present the standard multilevel QMC method in
Algorithm

4
, which exploits the fact that the extensible lattices and base
2
digital sequences we consider achieve nice space filling properties at samples sizes which are powers of
2
.

29.1.3
Fast Bayesian Multilevel Quasi-Monte Carlo Without Replications

We will assume
Y_{\ell}
is a Gaussian processes (GP)
[
51
]
and, while not necessarily being justified theoretically, we make the modeling assumption that
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{L}
are independent GPs. For now, let us consider a fixed
\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and drop
\ell
subscripts. At each level we prescribe
Y\sim\mathrm{GP}(\tau,K)
where
\tau
is a constant prior mean and
K
is the kernel which depends on hyperparameter
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{\gamma,{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\tau\}
(or
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{\gamma,{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\tau,{\boldsymbol{a}}\}
if using the adaptive smoothness kernel in
Definition

29.1
). For our fast GP constructions, we will use product kernels as in (
25
) which take the form

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\gamma\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1+\eta_{j}R(x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime}))

(57)

for some univariate kernel
R
. For lattices, our univariate kernels will take the form of
Definition

22.1
with
R:=K_{\alpha}
; we will use
\alpha=1
to assume minimal periodicity. For digital nets, we use a new digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) kernel of adaptive smoothness defined below. This adaptive DSI kernel takes a hyperparameter-weighted sum of individual DSI kernels
{\widetilde{R}}_{\alpha}
of various smoothness
\alpha
where
{\widetilde{R}}_{\alpha}
correspond to forms derived in
Section

20.3
.

Definition 29.1

(Adaptive smoothness digitally-shift-invariant (DSI) product kernel)
.

Our DSI kernels take the form of (
57
) using a weighted sum of univariate kernels with hyperparameter weights
{\boldsymbol{a}}>0
so that

\displaystyle R(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:={\widetilde{R}}(x\oplus x^{\prime})=a_{1}{\widetilde{R}}_{1}(x,x^{\prime})+a_{2}{\widetilde{R}}_{2}(x,x^{\prime})+a_{3}{\widetilde{R}}_{3}(x,x^{\prime})+a_{4}{\widetilde{R}}_{4}(x,x^{\prime})\quad\text{with }

(58)

\displaystyle{\widetilde{R}}_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}6\left(1-\frac{1}{2}t_{1}(x)\right),&\alpha=1\\
-1+-\beta(x)x+\frac{5}{2}\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right],&\alpha=2\\
-1+\beta(x)x^{2}-5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x+\frac{43}{18}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right],&\alpha=3\\
-1-\frac{2}{3}\beta(x)x^{3}+5\left[1-t_{1}(x)\right]x^{2}-\frac{43}{9}\left[1-t_{2}(x)\right]x\\
\quad+\frac{701}{294}\left[1-t_{3}(x)\right]+\beta(x)\left[\frac{1}{48}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{a}}{2^{3(a-1)}}-\frac{1}{42}\right],&\alpha=4\end{cases}.

Here
\beta(x)=-\lfloor\log_{2}(x)\rfloor
,
t_{\nu}(x)=2^{-\nu\beta(x)}
, and
x\oplus x^{\prime}=\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}(({\mathsf{x}}_{a}+{\mathsf{x}}_{a}^{\prime})\bmod 2)2^{-(a+1)}
is the exclusive or (XOR) between binary expansions of
x=\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}{\mathsf{x}}_{a}2^{-a}
and
x^{\prime}=\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}{\mathsf{x}}_{a}^{\prime}2^{-a}
as used for digital nets in
Section

11.2
.

For the fast Bayesian cubature discussed in
Section

24
, we have posterior distribution of the integral is the Gaussian random variable

\mu:={\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}[Y({\boldsymbol{X}})]\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\widehat{\mu}},{\widehat{V}}_{n}),\qquad{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}

where
{\widehat{\mu}}:={\mathbb{E}}[\mu|{\mathsf{X}},{\boldsymbol{Y}}]
and
{\widehat{V}}_{n}={\mathbb{V}}[\mu|{\mathsf{X}}]
for
{\mathsf{X}}:=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{Y}}:=(Y({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{n-1}
. Under our choice of product kernels built from univariate (digitally-)shift-invariant kernels, and assuming we optimize the prior mean
\tau
using the marginal log-likelihood (MLL) loss in
Section

21.3
, then we have

{\widehat{\mu}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}Y({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad{\widehat{V}}_{n}=\gamma\left[1-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}{\mathcal{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0})\right)^{-1}\right]

(59)

where
{\mathcal{K}}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(1+\eta_{j}R(x,x_{j}^{\prime})\right)
is the unscaled version of the product kernel in (
57
).

A key observation is that
{\widehat{V}}_{n}
only depends on the points
{\mathsf{X}}
, not the function evaluations
{\boldsymbol{Y}}
. This implies that, for fixed hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we may compute the projected variance
{\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}
for any
{\widehat{n}}\geq n
points from the given LD sequence. We prefer sample sizes
{\widehat{n}}
which are powers of two as there the LD sequences we consider attain desirable uniformity properties and enable fast GP computations. While one may compute
{\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}
exactly for any
{\widehat{n}}
, for later utility we will define
{\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}
when
{\widehat{n}}
is not a power of
2
to be the log-log linear interpolation between surrounding powers of two so that

{\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}:=\left(\frac{{\widehat{V}}_{2^{p}}^{p+1}}{{\widehat{V}}_{2^{p+1}}^{p}}\right){\widehat{n}}^{\log_{2}\left({\widehat{V}}_{2^{p+1}}/{\widehat{V}}_{2^{p}}\right)}\qquad\mathrm{when}\qquad 2^{p}<{\widehat{n}}<2^{p+1}\qquad\mathrm{for}\qquad p\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

(60)

with
p=\lfloor\log_{2}({\widehat{n}})\rfloor
, see
Figure

20
.

p
\log_{2}({\widehat{n}})
p+1
\log_{2}\left({\widehat{V}}_{2^{p}}\right)
\log_{2}\left({\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}\right)
\log_{2}\left({\widehat{V}}_{2^{p+1}}\right)

Figure 20
:
Log-log linear interpolation for
{\widehat{V}}_{\widehat{n}}
when
{\widehat{n}}
is not a power of
2
using
p=\lfloor\log_{2}({\widehat{n}})\rfloor
.

Let us now consider the multilevel Bayesian cubature setup where we fit independent GPs to each difference
Y_{\ell}
. We will select shifts
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{L}\in[0,1)^{d}
and use LD points
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i}={\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{\ell}
on each level. Here the shifts are arbitrary and need not be independent as was the case for MLQMC with replication in
Section

29.1.2
. One may even choose the same shift on each level,
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1}=\dots={\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{L}
, to use the same collocation points on each level as is common in recycling MLMC methods
[
179
,
178
]
. Different hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\ell}
will be fit on each level. Using (
59
) with level-dependent notations, we estimate
\nu
in (
54
) by the posterior cubature mean

{\widehat{\nu}}:={\mathbb{E}}[\nu|({\mathsf{X}}_{\ell},{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell}{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}\qquad\mathrm{where}\qquad{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}:={\mathbb{E}}[\mu_{\ell}|{\mathsf{X}}_{\ell},{\boldsymbol{Y}}_{\ell}]=\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}Y_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i}).

Additionally, under the assumption of independent GPs
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{L}
, the posterior cubature variance becomes

\displaystyle{\widehat{V}}_{n_{1},\dots,n_{L}}

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{V}}[\nu|({\mathsf{X}}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}{\widehat{V}}_{\ell,n_{\ell}}\quad\mathrm{where}\quad

(61)

\displaystyle{\widehat{V}}_{\ell,n_{\ell}}

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{V}}[\mu_{\ell}|{\mathsf{X}}_{\ell}]=\gamma_{\ell}\left[1-\left(\frac{1}{n_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{\ell}-1}{\mathcal{K}}_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell,0})\right)^{-1}\right]

with
{\mathcal{K}}_{\ell}
depending on hyperparameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\ell}\setminus\{\gamma\}
, i.e., all hyperparameters except the global scaling factor
\gamma
. If one is not willing to assume independent GPs
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{L}
, then the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality may be used to bound

{\mathbb{V}}[\nu|({\mathsf{X}}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}]\leq\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sqrt{{\widehat{V}}_{\ell,n_{\ell}}}\right)^{2}.

In our numerical experiments we use the independent GP assumption and take the standard error to be
{\widehat{V}}_{n_{1},\dots,n_{L}}
in (
61
) as we found it provided robust error estimation across our suite of test problems.

As was the case for MLQMC with replications in
Algorithm

4
, we will employ a scheme which iteratively doubles the sample size at selected levels. Our complete fast Bayesian MLQMC method is summarized in
Algorithm

6
which selects the level at which to double using the procedure in
Algorithm

5
. At each iteration, our selected level will depend on the projected variance
{\widehat{V}}_{\ell,{\widehat{n}}_{\ell}}
for certain
{\widehat{n}}_{\ell}
. Specifically, we compare subsequent levels in decreasing cost-of-doubling order and iteratively select the level with the greater decrease in projected variance for the same cost. Concretely, when comparing levels
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
, if
n_{\ell}C_{\ell}=n_{\ell^{\prime}}C_{\ell^{\prime}}
, i.e., the cost of doubling on each level is equivalent, then we choose to move forward with the level which gives the larger decrease in variance between
V_{\ell,n_{\ell}}-V_{\ell,2n_{\ell}}
and
V_{\ell^{\prime},n_{\ell^{\prime}}}-V_{\ell^{\prime},2n_{\ell^{\prime}}}
. If
n_{\ell}C_{\ell}>n_{\ell^{\prime}}C_{\ell^{\prime}}
, i.e., the cost of doubling on level
\ell
is greater than the cost of doubling on level
\ell^{\prime}
, then we choose to move forward with level which gives the larger decrease in variance between
V_{\ell,n_{\ell}}-V_{\ell,2n_{\ell}}
and
V_{\ell^{\prime},n_{\ell^{\prime}}-V_{\ell^{\prime},{\widehat{n}}_{\ell^{\prime}}}}
where
{\widehat{n}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is chosen to satisfy
n_{\ell}C_{\ell}=C_{\ell^{\prime}}({\widehat{n}}_{\ell^{\prime}}-n_{\ell^{\prime}})
so doubling the sample size on level
\ell
would require the same cost as increasing the sample size to
{\widehat{n}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
on level
\ell^{\prime}
. As
{\widehat{n}}_{\ell^{\prime}}
will usually not be a power of two, we use the log-log interpolation between surrounding powers of two as written in (
60
). The fact that the log-log linear interpolation recovers the case when
n_{\ell}C_{\ell}=n_{\ell^{\prime}}C_{\ell^{\prime}}
is reflected in the simplified presentation of
Algorithm

5
.

29.2
Numerical Experiments

In this section we present a number of numerical experiments for both single-level (Q)MC (
Section

29.2.1
) and multilevel (Q)MC (
Section

29.2.2
) using the three presented (Q)MC algorithms:

•

MC
(Multilevel) Monte Carlo with IID points as described in
Section

29.1.1
and
Algorithm

3
.

•

RQMC
(Multilevel) QMC with replications as described in
Section

29.1.2
and
Algorithm

4
.

•

BQMC
(Multilevel) Bayesian QMC without replications as described in

Section

29.1.3
and
Algorithm

6
.

Our Python implementation of these algorithms uses the fast Gaussian process regression package
FastGPs
from
Section

7
and the QMC software package
qmcpy

Section

6
. We will run
250
independent trials of each (multilevel) (Q)MC approximation for a given problem and budget. For QMC methods, the full text
[
17
]
considers both lattices and the digital nets. Here we will only present results for digital nets. We will always use the
joe-kuo-6.21201
digital net generating matrices from
https://web.maths.unsw.edu.au/~fkuo/sobol/index.html

[
136
,
137
]
. This is the default choices in
qmcpy
. Comparisons against other generating matrices is a valuable avenue for future work.

Most of the single-level test functions in
Section

29.2.1
were considered in
[
49
]
, albeit with the ridge functions having equal weights. There, comprehensive experiments showed that RQMC methods outperform alternative bootstrap methods in terms of confidence interval coverage across a range of benchmarks. Their experiments tested up to dimension
d=32
and only observed RQMC coverage failures when using
R=5
randomizations. We will use
R=8
replications in our RQMC testing. As
R
decreases, RQMC will typically yield better true errors with less accurate standard errors. We note that our choice of
R=8
is rather aggressive in comparison to existing literature which often uses at least
R=25
.

29.2.1
Single-Level Problems

Here we consider single-level test functions
Y
and run (Q)MC algorithms to estimate
\mu={\mathbb{E}}[Y({\boldsymbol{X}})]
for
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
and
d=32
. The ridge functions will be defined as
Y({\boldsymbol{x}})=g(u({\boldsymbol{x}}))
for
u({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{j}\Phi^{-1}(x_{j})
with weights
(c_{j})_{j=1}^{d}
and
\Phi
the CDF of the standard normal
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. We will only present results for
sparse weights

c_{j}=2^{-j}/\sqrt{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{d}2^{-2j^{\prime}}}
which make the problem QMC-friendly, but we observed similar findings for ridge functions with
equal weights

c_{j}=d^{-1/2}
. Both sparse and equal weights make
u({\boldsymbol{X}})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
when
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
. Our four test functions are listed below.

1.

sumxex:

Y({\boldsymbol{x}})=-d+\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}\exp(x_{j})
. This is a smooth and additive integrand which is easy for QMC methods.

2.

ridge PL sparse:

Y({\boldsymbol{x}})=g(u({\boldsymbol{x}}))
with
g(u)=\max\left(u-1,0\right)-\phi(1)+\Phi(-1)
and
\phi
the density of
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. This is a continuous piecewise linear (PL) function with a kink, a feature often observed in problems from computational finance.

3.

ridge JSU sparse:

Y({\boldsymbol{x}})=g(u({\boldsymbol{x}}))
with
g(u)=-\eta+F^{-1}\left(\Phi\left(v\right)\right)
where
F
is the CDF of a Johnson’s SU distribution
[
208
]
with parameters
\gamma=\delta=\lambda=1
and
\xi=0
, and
\eta
is the mean of that distribution. For
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
,
Y({\boldsymbol{X}})
has skewness
-5.66
and kurtosis
96.8
making it heavy tailed.

4.

Genz corner-peak 2:

Y({\boldsymbol{x}})=\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{j}x_{j}\right)^{-(d+1)}
with coefficients
c_{j}=j^{-2}/(4\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{d}(j^{\prime})^{-2})
. This is a corner-peak version of the Genz function
[
209
]
(as opposed to the oscillatory version) with coefficients of the second kind (out of three common options). The different coefficient options represent increasing levels of anisotropy and decreasing effective dimension.

In the top row of
Figure

21
we plot both the median true errors and median standard errors for single-level problems with an increasing budget
N=n
. For these single-level problems, the true error and standard error for IID Monte Carlo always converge at the expected
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
rate with the true errors closely matching the standard errors. The RQMC methods also yield standard errors which closely match true errors, with both converging faster than IID-MC methods. RQMC converges like
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-2})
for the easy sumxex problem, slightly below
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1})
for both the ridge PL sparse and ridge JSU sparse problems, and between
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-3/2})
for the Genz corner-peak 2 function. As expected, the BQMC methods almost always provide better true errors compared to the RQMC methods. In the majority of cases, the true error rates for BQMC match those for RQMC, but with smaller constant multiples. One exception is the true errors of BQMC-net for the sumxex problem which appears to converge at a rate slightly better than
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-3})
compared to the
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-2})
rate for the RQMC-net methods.

We found the BQMC standard errors are often able to match the true error convergence rates and provide slight improvements over the corresponding RQMC standard errors. However, the rate constants for the BQMC standard error are rather conservative for the true errors when higher-order convergence beyond
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-2})
is observed. For example, when applying BQMC-net to the easy sumxex problem, the true error converges like
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-3})
while the standard error converges with a rate around
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-2})
. One potential method to improve BQMC-net standard error convergence would be to expand the DSI sum kernels in
Definition

29.1
to consider even higher-order smoothness kernels beyond
\alpha=4
. However, evaluating higher-order smoothness kernels is more expensive in terms of both computations and memory, and a larger set of hyperparameters weights
{\boldsymbol{a}}
would need to be optimized. We also found the BQMC-net standard error convergence rate appears slightly worse than that for RQMC-net for the Genz corner-peak
2
function.

In
Figure

22
we plot the standard error and true error for each trial against each other. Again we see the conservative nature the BQMC standard error estimations in comparison to RQMC methods. This is especially evident for the sumxex and Genz corner-peak 2 functions at higher budgets
n
. For the two ridge functions, we found the BQMC standard errors were accurate for the true errors with both medians almost exactly matching.

Figure 21
:
Median true error and standard error versus budget for Monte Carlo (MC) with IID points, Quasi-Monte Carlo with replications (RQMC), and Quasi-Monte Carlo with fast Bayesian cubature (BQMC).

Figure 22
:
Standard error versus true error across trials for (single-level) Monte Carlo (MC) with IID points, Quasi-Monte Carlo with replications (RQMC), and Quasi-Monte Carlo with fast Bayesian cubature (BQMC). The stars represent the median true and standard errors for each budget.

Section

29.2.3
), solving a one-dimensional elliptic PDE (
Section

29.2.4
), and solving a two-dimensional nonlinear Darcy flow PDE (
Section

29.2.5
). Our multilevel numerics follow in
Section

29.2.6
.

10
gives the approximate means
\mu_{\ell}
and standard deviations
\sqrt{V_{\ell}}
for each problem. Our cost always increases with level, and we always normalize so that the cost on the maximum level is
1
.

29.2.3
Multilevel Option Pricing

Suppose we are given a financial option with starting price
S_{0}
, strike price
K
, interest rate
r
, and volatility
\sigma
which is exercised at time
1
. We use
S_{0}=K=100
,
r=0.05
, and
\sigma=0.2
. We will assume the asset path follows a geometric Brownian motion
S(t)=S_{0}e^{(r-\sigma^{2}/2)t+\sigma B(t)}
where
B(t)
is a standard Brownian motion. At level
\ell
, we monitor the asset at
d_{\ell}=2^{2+\ell}
times
(j/d_{\ell})_{j=1}^{d_{\ell}}
so that
(B(1/d_{\ell}),B(2/d_{\ell}),\dots,B(1))^{\intercal}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left({\boldsymbol{0}},{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{\ell}\right)
where
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{\ell}=\left(\min(j/d_{\ell},j^{\prime}/d_{\ell})\right)_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{d_{\ell}}
. We will use the eigendecomposition
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{\ell}={\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}^{\intercal}
to write
(B(1/d_{\ell}),B(2/d_{\ell}),\dots,B(1))^{\intercal}\sim{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}\Phi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{\ell})
where
\Phi^{-1}
is the inverse CDF of the standard normal applied elementwise to
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\ell}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d_{\ell}}
. The cost
C_{\ell}
on level
\ell
is proportional to
2^{\ell}
. We will consider the following two options with
L=8
levels of discretization.

1.

Asian Call Option with Geometric Averaging
Here the discounted fair prices of the infinite-dimensional options with continuous monitoring is

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max\left(\exp\left(\int_{0}^{1}\log(S(t))\mathrm{d}t\right)-K,0\right)\right]e^{-r}
, and for discrete monitoring at level
\ell
we use the finite-dimensional approximation

f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})=\max\left(\prod_{j=1}^{d_{\ell}}S(j/d_{\ell})-K,0\right)e^{-r}.

2.

Lookback Option
Here the discounted fair price of the infinite-dimensional option with continuous monitoring is
{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(1)-\min_{0\leq t\leq 1}S(t)\right]e^{-r}
, and for discrete monitoring at level
\ell
we use the finite-dimensional approximation

f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})=\left[S(1)-\min(S(1/d_{\ell}),S(2/d_{\ell}),\dots,S(1))\right]e^{-r}.

29.2.4
Multilevel Elliptic PDE

Let us consider the one-dimensional elliptic PDE
-\nabla(e^{a(u,{\boldsymbol{x}})}\nabla F(u,{\boldsymbol{x}}))=g(u)
with
u\in[0,1]
and boundary conditions
F(0,{\boldsymbol{x}})=F(1,{\boldsymbol{x}})=0
for all
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in[0,1]^{d}
. The forcing term
g
is set to the constant
g(u)=1
for all
u\in[0,1]
. We will generate
a(u,{\boldsymbol{X}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Phi^{-1}(X_{j})\sin(\pi ku)/j
with
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
in
d=8
dimensions and
\Phi
the CDF of the standard normal so
\Phi^{-1}(X_{j})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. Let us denote by
F_{\ell}
the level
\ell
numerical PDE solution using a finite difference method with
2^{1+\ell}+1
evenly spaced mesh points
(k/2^{1+\ell})_{k=0}^{2^{1+\ell}}
. We will take the discretized solution to be
f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})=F_{\ell}(1/2,{\boldsymbol{X}})
and use
L=4
levels. For each query of
f_{\ell}
we need to solve a tridiagonal linear system, which can be done with linear complexity. Therefore, we take the cost
C_{\ell}
on level
\ell
to be proportional to
2^{\ell}
.

29.2.5
Multilevel Nonlinear Darcy Flow PDE

Figure 23
:
Visualization of the Darcy flow PDE where each row represents a different realization
{\boldsymbol{x}}
of both the random conductivity and forcing term. The resolution increases with each of the
L=3
levels with the conductivity and forcing term shown at their maximum resolution. Here the quantity of interest on level
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
is
f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})=\max_{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}}F_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{X}})
for
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
.

We now consider a two-dimensional nonlinear Darcy flow problem

\begin{cases}-\nabla\cdot(e^{a({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}})}.\nabla F({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}}))+(F({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}}))^{3}=g({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}}),&{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{2}\\
F({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}})=0,&{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\partial[0,1]^{2}\end{cases}

where
g
is a forcing term and
e^{a}
represents the conductivity. Here we assume
g
and
a
are both random and independently drawn from Gaussian processes with the underlying stochasticity of both processes controlled by
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
. We will take
g
to be a draw from a GP with a
1/2
-Matérn kernel and
a
to be a draw from a GP with a Gaussian kernel. The exact parameterizations of these GPs can be found in the implementation code. As with the elliptic PDE example, we will let
F_{\ell}
denote the level
\ell
numerical PDE solution solved on a computational grid with
2^{2+\ell}-1
mesh points
(k/2^{2+\ell})_{k=1}^{2^{2+\ell}-1}
in each dimension. We numerically solve the PDE via iterative linearizations and a Levenberg–Marquardt scheme
[
210
,
211
]
. Our quantity of interest is
{\mathbb{E}}[\max_{\boldsymbol{u}}(F({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{x}}))]
, so we take the discretized solution to be
f_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{X}})=\max_{\boldsymbol{u}}F_{\ell}({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{X}})
with
{\boldsymbol{X}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
.
Figure

23
visualizes realizations of the conductivity, forcing term, and PDE solutions at varying levels of resolution. The numerical solver is run to machine precision backwards error. Theoretically, the cost of evaluating
f_{\ell}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(S_{\ell}2^{6\ell})
, with
S_{\ell}
the number of Levenberg–Marquardt steps, as at each step we need to solve a dense linear system in a
(2^{2+2\ell}-1)\times(2^{2+2\ell}-1)
matrix. Practically, we take
L=3
levels and set the costs proportional to the respective average runtimes
(4.2\times 10^{-5},9.9\times 10^{-5},2.8\times 10^{-3})
. Due to the large scale nature of this problem with
d=3844
dimensions, we chose to only consider the
\alpha=4
DSI kernel in
Definition

29.1
, i.e., we fixed
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\beta_{3}=0
and set
\beta_{4}=1
. This enabled faster hyperparameter optimization and reduced storage. Even so, our Darcy flow experiment required over
12
hours running in parallel on
5
NVIDIA A100 80 GB GPUs.

29.2.6
Results for Multilevel Numerical Experiments

In the bottom row of
Figure

21
we plot both the median true errors and median standard errors for multilevel problems with an increasing budget
N
. As was the case with the single-level problems, for these multilevel problems we again find IID Monte Carlo methods achieve matching true and standard errors with convergence like
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1/2})
for each problem. The RQMC methods also yielded accurate standard error estimates which closely matched the true errors for all problems. RQMC converged like
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1/2})
for both option pricing problems and the Darcy flow PDE with smaller rate constants than IID Monte Carlo in each case. For the elliptic PDE, RQMC converges like
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1})
. For BQMC we are able to accommodate smaller budgets
N
compared to the RQMC methods which require multiple replications, as evidence by the curves extending further to the left for BQMC than RQMC.

As with single-level problems, BQMC-net outperforms RQMC-net in terms of both true and standard errors in the vast majority of cases. We observed a better rate constant for BQMC-net than RQMC-net on both the Asian option and elliptic PDE examples. On the harder Lookback option and nonlinear Darcy examples we observed very similar errors between BQMC-net and RQMC-net. We did observe slightly better RQMC-net median true errors for the Darcy problem with the
N=2^{9}
and
N=2^{10}
budgets compared to BQMC-net, but BQMC-net then yields better true errors at our largest tested budgets of
N=2^{11}
and
N=2^{12}
. Due to the lack of higher-order convergence, we also found the BQMC-net standard error estimate to be accurate for the true errors, even in small sample regimes.

In
Figure

24
we plot the standard error and true error for each trial against each other. For BQMC methods we observed that as the budget increases the standard errors become tighter across the trials. In order words, for larger budgets
N
the BQMC methods become more consistent and confident in their standard error predictions. This feature is shared with IID Monte Carlo methods, but not with RQMC methods which always aggregate a fixed number of independent estimates
R
.

Finally, in
Figure

25
we plot the sample allocation versus budget. IID Monte Carlo often seems to allocate a greater proportion of the budget to lower levels in comparison to RQMC and BQMC methods. The RQMC and BQMC methods yielded similar allocations across most problems for both point sets. BQMC did show a slight preference for allocating more budget to moderate levels while RQMC tended to allocate a slightly greater proportion of the budget to the high and low levels.

Figure 24
:
Standard error versus true error across trials for multilevel Monte Carlo (MC) with IID points, Quasi-Monte Carlo with replications (RQMC), and Quasi-Monte Carlo with fast Bayesian cubature (BQMC). The stars represent the median true and standard errors for each budget.

Figure 25
:
Sample allocation by budget versus level for multilevel Monte Carlo (MC) with IID points, Quasi-Monte Carlo with replications (RQMC), and Quasi-Monte Carlo with fast Bayesian cubature (BQMC).

30
Gaussian Processes for Probability of Failure Estimation

This section follows
[
14
]
, a paper I worked on during my 2022 Givens Associate Intern appointment at Argonne National Laboratories in collaboration with Vishwas Rao.

Efficiently approximating the probability of system failure has gained increasing importance as expensive simulations begin to play a larger role in reliability quantification tasks in areas such as structural design, power grid design, and safety certification among others. This work derives credible intervals on the probability of failure for a simulation which we assume is a realization of a Gaussian process. We connect these intervals to binary classification error and comment on their applicability to a broad class of iterative schemes proposed throughout the literature. A novel iterative sampling scheme is proposed which can suggest multiple samples per batch for simulations with parallel implementations. We empirically test our scalable, open-source implementation on a variety of simulations including a Tsunami model where failure is quantified in terms of maximum wave height.

Specifically, the novel contributions of this section are as follows.

•

Provide credible intervals that hold with guaranteed confidence for schemes which derive probability of failure estimates from a probabilistic surrogate model.

•

Propose a sampling scheme for iteratively updating a Gaussian process surrogate to approximate probability of failure. This scheme is suitable to high performance computing (HPC) settings where the expensive simulation may be evaluated at multiple parameter configurations in parallel.

•

Provide a scalable, open-source implementation into the
QMCPy
library.

30.1
Binary Classification with Gaussian Processes

Suppose we have access to a simulation
g:[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
where we assume failure occurs whenever
g({\boldsymbol{u}})\geq 0
. Our goal is to quantify the probability of system failure subject to random parameters
{\boldsymbol{U}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
; see
Section

12
for a discussion on transformations to uniform randomness. We will model
g
as a Gaussian process (GP) with mean
M:[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and SPD covariance kernel
K:[0,1]^{d}\times[0,1]^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. The mean
M
and kernel
K
may correspond to either the prior or posterior distributions, see
Section

20.5
for discussion on GP regression.

Here we have two sources of uncertainty: there is uncertainty in the canonical
{\boldsymbol{U}}
on probability space
([0,1]^{d},{\mathcal{B}}([0,1]^{d}),{\mathbb{U}})
where
{\mathbb{U}}
is the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{d}
, and there is uncertainty in the canonical stochastic GP
g
on probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{G}})
indexed by elements in
[0,1]^{d}
. Here
{\mathbb{U}}
may be thought of as a “horizontal” probability over the parameter space while
{\mathbb{G}}
may be thought of as the “vertical” probability over the GP function space.

Let us define the random
horizontal failure and success regions

F,S:\Omega\to{\mathcal{B}}([0,1]^{d})
by

\displaystyle F(g)

\displaystyle:=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:g({\boldsymbol{u}})\geq 0\}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad S(g)

\displaystyle:=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:g({\boldsymbol{u}})<0\}

respectively. Then our quantity of interest is the
horizontal probability of failure

P:\Omega\to[0,1]
defined as

P(g):={\mathbb{U}}(F(g))={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})].

(62)

We will also define the
vertical probability of failure

p:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,1]
by

p({\boldsymbol{U}}):={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})]=\Phi\left(\frac{M({\boldsymbol{U}})}{\sigma({\boldsymbol{U}})}\right)

(63)

where
\Phi
is the CDF of a standard normal distribution and
\sigma^{2}({\boldsymbol{U}}):=K({\boldsymbol{U}},{\boldsymbol{U}})
is the variance of the GP at
{\boldsymbol{U}}
. The framework presented here applies to more general probabilistic models when the last equality above is replaced appropriately for non-Gaussian processes.

Let the deterministic
predicted horizontal failure and success regions

\hat{F},\hat{S}\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
be

\displaystyle\hat{F}

\displaystyle:=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:p({\boldsymbol{u}})\geq 1/2\}=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:M({\boldsymbol{u}})\geq 0\},

\displaystyle\hat{S}

\displaystyle:=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:p({\boldsymbol{u}})<1/2\}=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}\in[0,1]^{d}:M({\boldsymbol{u}})<0\}.

In other words,
\hat{F}
and
\hat{S}
are the regions where the GP mean fails and succeeds respectively. Then we may define the
true positive, true negative, false positive, and false negative
functions
\mathrm{TP},\mathrm{FP},\mathrm{TN},\mathrm{FN}:\Omega\to{\mathcal{B}}([0,1]^{d})
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{TP}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{F}\cap F(g),\qquad\mathrm{FP}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{F}\cap S(g),

\displaystyle\mathrm{TN}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{S}\cap S(g),\qquad\mathrm{FN}(g)

\displaystyle=\hat{S}\cap F(g).

Here positive indicates failure and negative indicates success. The bottom two panels of
Figure

26
visualize these four disjoint regions covering
[0,1]^{d}
for some
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Omega
.

Notice that
1_{\mathrm{TP}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\mathrm{TN}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\mathrm{FP}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\mathrm{FN}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})=1
. Then the
expected accuracy

\mathrm{ACC}:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,1]
is naturally defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{ACC}({\boldsymbol{U}})

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[1_{\mathrm{TP}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\mathrm{TN}(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]

\displaystyle=1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})p({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\hat{S}}({\boldsymbol{U}})[1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})]

\displaystyle=\max\{p({\boldsymbol{U}}),1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\},

and the
expected error rate

\mathrm{ERR}:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,1]
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{ERR}({\boldsymbol{U}})

\displaystyle:=1-\mathrm{ACC}({\boldsymbol{U}})

(64)

\displaystyle=1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})[1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})]+1_{\hat{S}}({\boldsymbol{U}})p({\boldsymbol{U}})

\displaystyle=\min\{p({\boldsymbol{U}}),1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\}.

The
\max
and
\min
expressions result from the fact that
{\boldsymbol{U}}\in\hat{F}
if and only if
p({\boldsymbol{U}})\geq 1/2
.

Figure 26
:
The top figure shows the true simulation and a Gaussian process fit to a few data points. The bottom two plots show some Gaussian process sample paths
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Omega
with their corresponding
\mathrm{TP}
,
\mathrm{FP}
,
\mathrm{TN}
, and
\mathrm{FN}
regions (true positive, false positive, true negative, and false negative regions). The predicted failure
\mathrm{TP}
and
\mathrm{FP}
regions are shown along the top of each plot while the predicted success
\mathrm{TN}
and
\mathrm{FN}
regions are shown along the bottom of each plot.

30.2
Estimators and Credible Intervals

Our main contributions are
Theorem

30.1
and
Theorem

30.2
in this section which derive error bounds for the horizontal probability of failure quantity of interest
P(g)
in (
62
). Specifically, we derive credible intervals of the form
[\underline{P},\overline{P}]
so that

{\mathbb{G}}\left(P\in[\underline{P},\overline{P}]\right)\geq 1-\alpha.

(65)

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
is some uncertainty threshold.

Theorem 30.1

(Credible Interval from Posterior Mean Estimate)
.

Denote the posterior mean estimate by

{\widecheck{P}}:={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}[P(g)]={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})]]={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[p({\boldsymbol{U}})].

(66)

Then (
65
) holds when

\underline{P}=\max\left\{{\widecheck{P}}-\check{\gamma},0\right\},\qquad\overline{P}=\min\left\{{\widecheck{P}}+\check{\gamma},1\right\},\qquad\check{\gamma}:=\frac{2{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[p({\boldsymbol{U}})(1-p({\boldsymbol{U}}))\right]}{\alpha}.

(67)

Proof.

Markov’s inequality, Jensen’s inequality, and the triangle inequality imply that for
\gamma>0
we have

\displaystyle\gamma{\mathbb{G}}\left(\left\lvert P-{\widecheck{P}}\right\rvert\geq\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert P-{\widecheck{P}}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})]-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[p({\boldsymbol{U}})]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})\left(1-p(U)\right)-1_{S(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})\left(1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right)+1_{S(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\left(1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right)+{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[1_{S(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]

\displaystyle=2{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[p({\boldsymbol{U}})\left(1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right)\right].

∎

Theorem 30.2

(Credible Interval from Predicted Failure Region Estimate)
.

Denote the probability of the predicted failure region estimate by

\hat{P}:={\mathbb{U}}(\hat{F})={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})].

(68)

Then (
65
) holds when

\underline{P}=\max\left\{\hat{P}-\hat{\gamma},0\right\},\qquad\overline{P}=\min\left\{\hat{P}+\hat{\gamma},1\right\},\qquad\hat{\gamma}:=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[\mathrm{ERR}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]}{\alpha}

(69)

for
\mathrm{ERR}({\boldsymbol{U}})=\min\{p({\boldsymbol{U}}),1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\}
as defined in (
64
).

Proof.

Markov’s inequality, Jensen’s inequality, and the triangle inequality imply that for
\gamma>0
we have

\displaystyle\gamma{\mathbb{G}}\left(\left\lvert P-\hat{P}\right\rvert\geq\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert P-\hat{P}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})-1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})1_{\hat{S}}({\boldsymbol{U}})-1_{S(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})1_{\hat{S}}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{S(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})1_{\hat{F}}(U)\right]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}\left[1_{F(g)}({\boldsymbol{U}})1_{\hat{S}}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\bar{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]1_{\hat{F}}({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{G}}[1_{\mathrm{FN}}({\boldsymbol{U}})+1_{\mathrm{FP}}({\boldsymbol{U}})]]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[\mathrm{ERR}({\boldsymbol{U}})].

∎

Since for any
{\boldsymbol{U}}\in[0,1]^{d}
either
p({\boldsymbol{U}})\geq 1/2
or
1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\geq 1/2
, we see that
\min\{p({\boldsymbol{U}}),1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\}\leq 2p({\boldsymbol{U}})\left(1-p({\boldsymbol{U}})\right)
. Therefore, the credible interval in
Theorem

30.2
is tighter than the credible interval in
Theorem

30.1
, and going forward we take
[\underline{P},\overline{P}]
to be defined as in (
69
).

30.3
Adaptive Algorithm

Our proposed algorithm sequentially updates a surrogate GP to refine the probability of failure estimate and shrink the resulting credible interval. The general iterative procedure is as follows.

1.

Input
a GP prior distribution
{\mathbb{G}}_{0}
specified by a prior mean function
M_{0}
and prior covariance function
K_{0}
.

2.

Input
a simulation
g
which we assume is a realization of the GP. The prior number of samples of
g
is
n\leftarrow 0
.

3.

Step 1
Evaluate
g
at some batch of nodes
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}
and set
n\leftarrow n+b
.

4.

Step 2
Based on all previous evaluations of
g
, update the GP posterior distribution to
{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
with posterior mean
M_{n}
and posterior covariance function
K_{n}
.

5.

Step 3
Compute
N
-sample Quasi-Monte Carlo (see
Section

8
) approximations
\hat{P}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n}
and
\hat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n}
of
\hat{P}_{n}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
respectively as defined in (
68
), (
69
) based on the posterior distribution
{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
.

6.

Step 4
If the approximate
1-\alpha
credible interval

[\underline{P}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n},\overline{P}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n}]:=[\max\{\hat{P}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n}-\hat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n},0\},\min\{\hat{P}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n}+\hat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{QMC}}_{n},1\}]

is desirably narrow or the budget for sampling
g
has expired, we are done. Otherwise, return to Step 1 to continue refining the approximate estimate and credible interval.

Despite not being written explicitly, the estimates
\hat{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{QMC}}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{n}^{\mathrm{QMC}}
in Step 3 depend on the desired uncertainty threshold
\alpha
, the posterior distribution
{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
, and the
N
QMC sampling nodes
{\mathsf{U}}_{N}:=\{{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{N-1}
. We choose to leave the low-discrepancy nodes
{\mathsf{U}}_{N}
unchanged across iterations.
N
is chosen to be large, e.g.,
N=2^{20}
, as the QMC estimates are computed using only the posterior mean
M_{n}
and posterior covariance
K_{n}
which are relatively cheap to evaluate.

The choice of sampling scheme in Step 1 is arbitrary. A number of deterministic one step (
b=1
) look ahead schemes are proposed in
[
76
,
212
,
213
,
214
,
215
,
216
,
74
,
217
,
218
]
. While some are theoretically applicable to
b>1
, the optimization required in practice quickly becomes intractable. We wish to allow
b>1
so parallel implementations of simulations on HPC systems may be fully utilized.

To this end, we propose to sample
b
IID points from the distribution with unnormalized density
2\mathrm{ERR}_{n}\leq 1
as defined in (
64
) under
{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
. We use rejection sampling
[
219
,
220
,
221
]
to draw IID samples from the unnormalized density
\varrho=2\mathrm{ERR}_{n}
, see
Algorithm

7
. The random number of tries
T
required to draw a single sample from density
\varrho
with rejection sampling follows a geometric distribution
T\sim\mathrm{Geom}(c)
where
c={\mathbb{E}}\left[\varrho({\boldsymbol{U}})\right]
is equivalent to the rejection sampling efficiency with
{\boldsymbol{U}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
. Therefore, the expected number of tries required to draw
b
IID points from
2\mathrm{ERR}_{n}
is
b/(2{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{U}}[\mathrm{ERR}_{n}({\boldsymbol{U}})])=b/(2\alpha\hat{\gamma}_{n})
. This randomized, non-greedy scheme shows strong empirical performance in the next section.

Algorithm 7

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{AlgRS}}(\varrho,b)
: Rejection Sampling

\varrho:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,1]

\triangleright
unnormalized density to sample from

b\in{\mathbb{N}}

\triangleright
the number of samples to draw

{\mathsf{X}}_{0}\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright
initialize an empty point set of IID draws from
\varrho

i\leftarrow 0

\triangleright
initialize accepted counter

t\leftarrow 0

\triangleright
initialize tried counter

while

i<b

do

draw
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
independent of
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{j}:j<t\},\{{\boldsymbol{U}}_{j}:j<t\}

\triangleright
draw candidate

draw
{\boldsymbol{U}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{U}}(0,1)
independent of
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{j}:j\leq t\},\{{\boldsymbol{U}}_{j}:j<t\}

\triangleright
draw threshold

if

{\boldsymbol{U}}_{t}\leq\varrho({\boldsymbol{X}}_{t})

then

i\leftarrow i+1

\triangleright
increment accepted counter

{\mathsf{X}}_{i}\leftarrow{\mathsf{X}}_{i-1}\cup\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t}\}

\triangleright
add candidate to the accepted set

t\leftarrow t+1

\triangleright
increment tried counter

return

{\mathsf{X}}_{b}

\triangleright
return the
b
IID draws from
\varrho

30.4
Numerical Experiments

The following numerical experiments are based on our open-source Python implementation in
QMCPy
.
Since
\mathrm{ERR}_{0}\propto 1_{[0,1]^{d}}
, a uniform density, the initial samples are selected from a randomized low-discrepancy sequence rather than drawing IID points using rejection sampling as indicated in the algorithm. As discussed in
Section

21.2
, the prior mean
M_{0}
and kernel
K_{0}
often involve hyperparameters which may be optimized to better fit the data. We will use the Matérn kernels in
Definition

22.4
which have parameters for smoothness, lengthscales, and a global scale. Our implementation allows one to either re-optimize hyperparameters at each iteration or keep them fixed after a certain number of iterations.

We consider a number of benchmark examples in small and medium dimensions. These are listed in
Table

11
alongside their true solution and corresponding figure reference. Such examples are often defined by an original function
\tilde{g}:{\mathcal{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with respect to non-uniform random variable
{\boldsymbol{V}}
on
{\mathcal{T}}
and where failure occurs when the simulation exceeds some
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We may perform a change of variables so
{\boldsymbol{V}}\sim\varphi({\boldsymbol{U}})
where
{\boldsymbol{U}}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]^{d}
as desired and the probability of failure of
g({\boldsymbol{U}}):=\tilde{g}(\varphi({\boldsymbol{U}}))-\xi
is equivalent to the probability of failure of
\tilde{g}({\boldsymbol{V}})
. See
Section

12
or
[
5
]
for a more rigorous variable transformation framework and further examples.

As a more realistic example, we look at the probability the maximum height of a Tsunami exceeds

.

In all the examples we see the credible interval captures the true mean and the width of the credible interval decreases steadily across iterations. The true error is usually at least one order of magnitude below the error indicated by the credible interval. Notice the desirable behavior where the samples begin to cluster near the failure boundary in later iterations. We found results to be highly dependent on the choice of the prior kernel, prior mean, and their hyperparameters.
